Statistical Inference After Adaptive Sampling for Longitudinal Data
Kelly W. Zhang, Lucas Janson, Susan A. Murphy

Abstract
Online reinforcement learning and other adaptive sampling algorithms are increasingly used in digital intervention experiments to optimize treatment delivery for users over time. In this work, we focus on longitudinal user data collected by a large class of adaptive sampling algorithms that are designed to optimize treatment decisions online using accruing data from multiple users. Combining or “pooling” data across users allows adaptive sampling algorithms to potentially learn faster. However, by pooling, these algorithms induce dependence between the sampled user data trajectories; we show that this can cause standard variance estimators for i.i.d. data to underestimate the true variance of common estimators on this data type. We develop novel methods to perform a variety of statistical analyses on such adaptively sampled data via Z-estimation. Specifically, we introduce the adaptive sandwich variance estimator, a corrected sandwich estimator that leads to consistent variance estimates under adaptive sampling. Additionally, to prove our results we develop novel theoretical tools for empirical processes on non-i.i.d., adaptively sampled longitudinal data which may be of independent interest. This work is motivated by our efforts in designing experiments in which online reinforcement learning algorithms optimize treatment decisions, yet statistical inference is essential for conducting analyses after experiments conclude.



1
Introduction

Online reinforcement learning (RL) and other adaptive sampling algorithms are increasingly used in digital intervention experiments to optimize treatment delivery for users over time
Cai et al. 2021
;
Figueroa et al. 2021
;
Forman et al. 2019
;
Liao et al. 2020
;
Piette et al. 2022
;
Trella et al. 2022
;
Trella et al. 2023
;
Yom-Tov et al. 2017
. For example, in mobile health, online RL algorithms have been used in longitudinal clinical trials developing Just-In-Time interventions for people with a variety of chronic health problems
Figueroa et al. 2021
;
Liao et al. 2020
;
Piette et al. 2022
;
Yom-Tov et al. 2017
.
These trials are longitudinal because they involve making multiple treatment decisions for users over time. Online RL algorithms are used during the experiment to optimize treatment decisions; specifically, the RL algorithm uses the outcomes of previous treatment decisions to inform future treatment selection. A critical consideration when designing experiments that use such RL algorithms is ensuring that one can use the resulting data collected to perform valid statistical inference after the experiment is over. For example, one might want to construct confidence intervals for a treatment effect on a variety of different outcomes, including the reward. This work is motivated by experimental trials for digital health interventions in which RL algorithms are used to optimize treatments over time for multiple users. In these settings, valid post-trial statistical inference is critical to inform decisions about whether to roll out or how to improve a given digital intervention after the trial is over
Figueroa et al. 2021
;
Liao et al. 2020
;
Trella et al. 2022
. A significant challenge is to develop valid statistical inference methods that are applicable to the data collected by the variety of RL algorithms that those designing digital interventions want to use.

Recently in the longitudinal digital intervention space, there has been great interest in online RL algorithms that combine or “pool” data across multiple users to inform future treatment decisions, because they can potentially learn faster how best to select treatments. In fact, there is so much interest that several digital health intervention trials have already used such pooling RL algorithms
Figueroa et al. 2021
;
Piette et al. 2022
;
Tomkins et al. 2021
;
Yom-Tov et al. 2017
.
However, it is unclear whether existing statistical inference methods for longitudinal data
Boruvka et al. 2018
;
Fitzmaurice et al. 2012
;
Qian et al. 2021
;
Robins 1997
;
Zeger and Liang 1986
, which assume independent user data trajectories, should be used on data collected with adaptive sampling algorithms that pool data online. This is because, by pooling, adaptive sampling algorithms induce dependence between the collected user data trajectories. For example, if the algorithm uses the outcomes of one user to inform future treatment decisions for another user, the data trajectories collected from these two users will not be independent.

There are existing approaches for statistical inference after adaptive sampling that account for the dependence induced by the algorithm. However, these approaches make a variety of restrictive assumptions on how users’ outcomes can evolve over time and can be affected by treatments. For example, many works assume a classical contextual bandit environment in which user states are i.i.d. over time and the mean reward only depends on the most recent state and treatment
Bibaut et al. 2021a
;
Bibaut et al. 2021b
;
Chen et al. 2020
;
Deshpande et al. 2018
;
Hadad et al. 2021
;
Hu and Rosenberger 2006
;
Zhan et al. 2021
;
Zhang et al. 2020
;
Zhang et al. 2021
.
These inference approaches are not applicable to classical longitudinal data settings in which (a) treatment decisions may affect users’ future responsiveness to treatments or (b) user outcomes may be non-stationary.

Moreover, we want our statistical inference approach to be robust to misspecification of the model used by the RL algorithm. Specifically, online RL algorithms make treatment decisions using approximate models for the users’ outcomes (i.e., models of the environment) that they repeatedly fit using the data collected during the experiment. The models used in online RL algorithms are chosen to appropriately trade off bias and variance so the algorithm can quickly learn to select effective treatments. For example, even in environments in which treatment decisions may impact users’ responsiveness to treatments multiple decision times into the future, in order to reduce variance, often simpler algorithms that do not model these delayed effects of treatment (like bandit algorithms) are preferred
Figueroa et al. 2021
;
Trella et al. 2022
;
Yom-Tov et al. 2017
. After the digital intervention experiment is over, we argue that the validity of the statistical inference using the resulting adaptively sampled longitudinal data should not require that these approximate models used by the online RL algorithm are correctly specified.

1.1
Our Contribution

In this work, we consider pooling adaptive sampling algorithms that, for each decision time
t\in[1\colon T]
, form a policy
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}
that appropriately converges to a
target
policy
\pi_{t}^{*}
as the number of users
n
grows; see Remark
2
and Section
3.2
for further discussion of this assumption. We provide statistical theory for Z-estimators
(
Van der Vaart 2000
, Chapter 5)
on data collected by such pooling adaptive sampling algorithms. Z-estimators encompass most classical statistical estimators (e.g., least squares and maximum likelihood estimators) and are often used in estimating time-varying causal effects
Robins 1997
. We derive the asymptotic distribution of these Z-estimators as the number of users
n\to\infty
to facilitate the construction of asymptotically valid confidence regions. Specifically, we prove that the commonly used standard sandwich variance estimator
Huber 1967
;
Zeileis 2006
, can underestimate the true variance of Z-estimators when data is adaptively sampled via algorithms that learn by pooling data across users. We develop the
adaptive sandwich estimator
, a corrected sandwich estimator that leads to consistent variance estimates under adaptive sampling. Specifically, our contributions are as follows:

1.

Facilitating Statistical Inference after Using Pooling Adaptive Sampling Algorithms on Longitudinal Data:

Our approach for inference via Z-estimators is the first method that is applicable to longitudinal datasets collected by adaptive sampling algorithms that learn by pooling data across users. Moreover, the validity of our approach does not require the approximate outcome models learned by the adaptive sampling algorithm to be correct. This work enables digital intervention researchers to use pooling adaptive sampling algorithms in their experiments without sacrificing the statistical validity in performing a wide variety of after-study analyses.

2.

Novel use of Radon-Nikodym Derivatives to Facilitate Theory for Adaptively Sampled Data:

A significant technical challenge is that standard methods for empirical processes are insufficient for proving our asymptotic normality results since the adaptively sampled user data trajectories are not i.i.d. A key approach we use to facilitate theory for this non-i.i.d. data type is Radon-Nikodym derivative weighting. Specifically, we consider settings in which the estimator of the parameter of interest and the estimators used by the adaptive sampling policies
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}
are each a solution to some standard estimating function. Incorporating Radon-Nikodym derivative weights is integral to defining
joint
estimating functions for the parameter of interest and the policy parameters. Note that the joint estimating functions (and the Radon-Nikodym derivative weights) are used
solely
to analyze the asymptotic distribution of these estimators and
not
needed to form the estimators themselves. We introduce these weights in Section
5.1
.

3.

Empirical Process Theory for Adaptively Sampled Longitudinal Data:
To prove our results we develop novel theoretical tools for empirical processes on non-i.i.d., adaptively sampled longitudinal data, which may be of independent interest. These empirical processes are weighted by the Radon-Nikodym derivatives mentioned earlier. Specifically, we develop a Weighted Martingale Central Limit Theorem for functions of adaptively sampled data (Theorem
D.4
), as well as a novel Weighted Martingale Bernstein Inequality (Lemma
E.2
). Using these two results, we prove a functional asymptotic normality result for Radon-Nikodym derivative weighted empirical processes under bracketing number conditions. See Section
5.1.2
for more details.

2
Preliminaries

We consider a batch dataset collected by an adaptive sampling algorithm that pools across users. The dataset is comprised of data on
n
users over
T
decision times. For each decision time
t\in[1\colon T]
and user
i\in[1\colon n]
, the observations consist of a multi-dimensional vector of random variables which we call the state,
S_{t}^{(i)}\in\real^{d_{S}}
; a scalar action (i.e., treatment),
A_{t}^{(i)}\in\mathcal{A}
(here
\mathcal{A}
is a finite set, so
|\mathcal{A}|<\infty
); and lastly the multi-dimensional outcome vector of random variables,
Y_{t}^{(i)}\in\real^{d_{Y}}
. Often adaptive sampling algorithms are designed to maximize a reward; in this case, the reward
R_{t}^{(i)}\in\real
is some known function of the outcome vector
Y_{t}^{(i)}
. We define
Y_{t}^{(i)}
because often we are interested in inference regarding quantities that are not the reward. For example, the reward in a physical activity digital health study could be the user’s step count, but we may be interested in other outcomes like the user’s heart rate.

We use potential outcomes
Imbens and Rubin 2015
to represent counter-factual outcomes. We consider a longitudinal data setting in which the potential outcomes for
Y_{t}^{(i)}
may depend on all actions taken on user
i
up to decision time
t
,
A_{1:t}^{(i)}
; we use the notation
A_{1:t}^{(i)}\triangleq\big\{A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}\big\}_{t^{\prime}=1}^{t}
to denote collections of random variables. This means
Y_{t}^{(i)}
has
|\mathcal{A}|^{t}
different potential outcomes,
\big\{Y_{t}^{(i)}(a_{1:t}):a_{1:t}\in\mathcal{A}^{t}\big\}
, where
\mathcal{A}^{t}
denotes the
t
-fold Cartesian product of
\mathcal{A}
. Similarly, states have potential outcomes
\big\{S_{t}^{(i)}(a_{1:t-1}):a_{1:t-1}\in\mathcal{A}^{t-1}\big\}
. The observed variables are
Y_{t}^{(i)}\triangleq Y_{t}^{(i)}\big(A_{1:t}^{(i)}\big)
and
S_{t}^{(i)}\triangleq S_{t}^{(i)}\big(A_{1:t-1}^{(i)}\big)
.

We consider the setting in which the potential outcomes,
i\in[1\colon n]
, are i.i.d. according to an unknown
\mathcal{P}
, i.e.,

D^{(i)}=\left\{S_{t}^{(i)}(a_{1:t-1}),Y_{t}^{(i)}(a_{1:t}):a_{1:t}\in\mathcal{A}^{t}\right\}_{t=1}^{T}\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}\mathcal{P},~\textnormal{ i.i.d over users }i\in[1\colon n].

(2.1)

Note that the above allows for the trajectory of the observed user states and outcomes to be non-stationary and dependent over time. This setting encompasses both Markovian and non-Markovian user environments and is widely used in the longitudinal data analysis literature
Fitzmaurice et al. 2012
;
Robins 1986
;
Robins 1997
.

We consider adaptively sampled data for which at decision time
t=1
, a pre-specified policy
\pi_{1}
, where
\mathbb{P}\big(A_{1}^{(i)}\big|S_{1}^{(i)}\big)\triangleq\pi_{1}\big(A_{1}^{(i)},S_{1}^{(i)}\big)
, is used to select the treatment actions independently for all users. Then, for each
t\geq 2
, an adaptive sampling algorithm may use all the observed data so far across all users to form a policy
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}
.
Specifically the policy
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}
can be formed using the history
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(i)}\triangleq\big\{S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},Y_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}\big\}_{t^{\prime}=1}^{t-1}
for all users
i\in[1\colon n]
; for convenience we will use the notation
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(1:n)}\triangleq\big\{\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(i)}\big\}_{i=1}^{n}
to represent the collective history for all users.

The policy
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}
takes as input the user’s current state,
S_{t}^{(i)}
, and outputs a sampling probability distribution over the action space
\mathcal{A}
. For
a\in\mathcal{A}
,

\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}\big(a,S_{t}^{(i)}\big)=\mathbb{P}\left(A_{t}^{(i)}=a\big|S_{t}^{(i)},\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(1:n)}\right).

(2.2)

We consider data collected by adaptive sampling algorithms that, conditional on
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(1:n)}
and
S_{t}^{(1:n)}
, select actions
A_{t}^{(1)},A_{t}^{(2)},\dots,A_{t}^{(n)}
independently using policy
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}
. Note that the actions are not identically distributed conditional on
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(1:n)}
as the realized value of users’ states at time
t
may differ.

Remark 1

(Dependent User Data Trajectories)
.

Note that even though the users’ potential outcomes
D^{(i)}
are i.i.d. (as seen in display (
2.1
)), the observed user data trajectories,
\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)}
, are generally not independent over
i\in[1\colon n]
due to algorithm’s use of the common history,
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(1:n)}
in sampling the actions
A_{t}^{(1)},A_{t}^{(2)},\dots,A_{t}^{(n)}
.

For our statistical analyses, we consider asymptotics as the number of users,
n
, goes to infinity and keep the total number of decision times,
T
, fixed.
This decision is motivated by our work in digital intervention experiments. These experiments are primarily concerned with using inference methods to draw scientific conclusions about a population of individuals over a fixed period of time, e.g., a
90
-day physical activity mobile health intervention for individuals with stage-1 hypertension
Liao et al. 2020
.

We now informally provide several key assumptions that we make on the online pooling adaptive sampling algorithm used to collect the data (see Section
3.2
for more details). We assume that policies
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}
belong to a parametric class

\left\{\pi_{t}\big(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t-1}\big):\beta_{t-1}\in\real^{d_{t-1}}\right\}.

In particular,
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}(\,\cdot\,)\triangleq\pi_{t}\big(\,\cdot\,;\hat{\beta}_{t-1}^{(n)}\big)
, where
\hat{\beta}_{t-1}^{(n)}
is a function of all users’ data prior to time
t
,
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(1:n)}
. For a given action
a\in\mathcal{A}
and state
s\in\real^{d_{S}}
,
\pi_{t}\big(a,s;\hat{\beta}_{t-1}^{(n)}\big)
is a probability of selecting action
a
conditional on state
s
.
We will assume conditions under which
\hat{\beta}_{t-1}^{(n)}
converges to a deterministic
\beta_{t-1}^{*}
as the number of users
n\to\infty
. Hence, we call
\pi_{t}(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t-1}^{*})
, which we abbreviate as
\pi_{t}^{*}
, the
target policy
at time
t
.

Remark 2

(Target Policies)
.

Note that the assumption that there exists a target policy
\pi_{t}^{*}
for each decision time
t
is rather mild; this is because the asymptotic arguments derived here are as
n\to\infty
with the total number of decision times
T
fixed. Further,
\beta_{t-1}^{*}
can be an arbitrary deterministic limit, e.g.,
\beta_{t-1}^{*}
does not have to be a parameter in a correctly specified model of the reward, and
\pi_{t}^{*}
does not have to be optimal in any way. We also allow the target policy
\pi_{t}^{*}
to change with
t\in[2\colon T]
; this allows for non-stationarity in the users’ outcomes that is not accounted for by the algorithm. In the special case that the environment is stationary and any models assumed by the algorithm are correctly specified, the target policy
\pi_{t}^{*}
may be the same for all
t\in[2\colon T]
.
See Section
3.2
for more on the assumptions made on the policies.

3
Problem Statement

3.1
Inference Objective

We consider estimands that are defined with respect to the distribution in which the target policies
\pi_{2:T}^{*}\triangleq\big\{\pi_{t}^{*}\big\}_{t=2}^{T}
are used to select actions.
Specifically, we aim to conduct inference about a parameter
\theta^{*}
that solves

\bm{0}=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\left[\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta\big)\right],

(3.1)

where
\psi
is a measurable function of
\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)}
indexed by a finite-dimensional
\theta\in\real^{d_{\theta}}
.

The above expectation is indexed by the target policies
\pi_{2:T}^{*}
to indicate that the expectation is over the distribution of
\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)}
in which the actions are selected using the target policies and user potential outcomes are drawn from
\mathcal{P}
as described in display (
2.1
); we will use
\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}}
to refer to this distribution. Note that when the target policies
\pi_{2:T}^{*}
are used to select actions, the data is no longer “adaptively sampled” so the user trajectories
\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)}
are i.i.d.; thus,
\theta^{*}
is not indexed by
i
.

To estimate
\theta^{*}
we use Z-estimation; the estimator
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
satisfies

o_{P}\big(1/\sqrt{n}\big)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\hat{\theta}^{(n)}\big).

(3.2)

This setup encompasses many types of standard estimators (e.g., least squares and maximum likelihood) and includes minimizers of differentiable loss functions.
We are interested in constructing confidence regions for
\theta^{*}
. We do this by characterizing the asymptotic distribution of
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
as the number of users
n\to\infty
and using the asymptotic distribution to approximate the finite-sample distribution of
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
.

To enhance expositional clarity we illustrate the ideas using the running example of a least squares estimator in a binary action setting,
\mathcal{A}=\{0,1\}
, with the following
\psi
:

\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta\big)=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(Y_{t}^{(i)}-\theta_{0}^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}-A_{t}^{(i)}\theta_{1}^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\right)\begin{bmatrix}S_{t}^{(i)}\\
A_{t}^{(i)}S_{t}^{(i)}\end{bmatrix}.

(3.3)

Above,
Y_{t}^{(i)}\in\real
,
\theta=[\theta_{0},\theta_{1}]
, and the first entry of
S_{t}^{(i)}
is
1
(intercept term) for all
t,i
.

Remark 3

(Interpretation when the Model used by
\psi
is Misspecified)
.

Often in Z-estimation,
\psi
corresponds to the derivative of a likelihood function for a parameter in a particular (possibly semi- or non-parametric) model for the data; in this case we can think of
\psi
as “correctly specified” if that model holds in our data. In the least squares example,
\psi
is correctly specified if
\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{t}^{(i)}\big|S_{t}^{(i)},A_{t}^{(i)},\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(i)}\big]=\theta_{0}^{*,\top}S_{t}^{(i)}+A_{t}^{(i)}\theta_{1}^{*,\top}S_{t}^{(i)}
a.s. for all
t
. As is standard for Z-estimators, if
\psi
is not correctly specified, then
\theta^{*}
is the best
projected
solution; for example, in the least squares example from display (
3.3
),
\theta^{*}
corresponds to the best fitting linear model. The projection is with respect to the distribution
\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}}
in which target policies
\pi_{2:T}^{*}
are used to select actions. In this case
\theta^{*}
is a function of the target policies
\pi_{2:T}^{*}
. However in the correctly specified model case,
\theta^{*}
does not depend on the target policies. See Section
5.4
for how correct model specification affects the adaptive sandwich variance.

3.1.1
Excursion Effects are a Key Use Case

Excursion effects, which are used for the primary analysis in micro-randomized trials
Boruvka et al. 2018
;
Qian et al. 2022
;
Qian et al. 2021
, are a key use case for our inference method. In these longitudinal trials, treatment actions for each individual are repeatedly randomized using stochastic policies. The primary analysis for these trials concerns treatment effect excursions from the experiment’s target policies. An example excursion effect is the following excursion from the target policy at time
t
:

\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t-1}^{*}}\left[Y_{t}^{(i)}\big(A_{1:t-1}^{(i)},a_{t}=1\big)-Y_{t}^{(i)}\big(A_{1:t-1}^{(i)},a_{t}=0\big)\right].

(3.4)

In the simplified setting in which the outcome
Y_{t}^{(i)}
only depends on the most recent action
A_{t}^{(i)}
, the excursion effect simplifies to the standard treatment effect

\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{t}^{(i)}(a_{t}=1)-Y_{t}^{(i)}(a_{t}=0)\big].

Thus, the excursion effect from display (
3.4
) can be considered a generalization of the standard treatment effect to environments in which all actions taken so far,
A_{1:t}^{(i)}
, can affect the distribution of the outcome
Y_{t}^{(i)}
.

3.2
Policies Formed by the Adaptive Sampling Algorithm

As discussed in Section
2
(Preliminaries), at each decision time
t\in[2\colon T]
, the adaptive sampling algorithm uses all previously observed user data,
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(1:n)}
, to form a policy
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}
and uses this policy to select actions. In particular, we assume that there are policy function classes

\left\{\pi_{t}\big(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t-1}\big):\beta_{t-1}\in\real^{d_{t-1}}\right\}

(3.5)

for each
t\in[2\colon T]
and that
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}(\,\cdot\,)\triangleq\pi_{t}(\,\cdot\,;\hat{\beta}_{t-1}^{(n)})
where
\hat{\beta}_{t-1}^{(n)}\in\real^{d_{t-1}}
is a statistic formed using
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(1:n)}
.

Recall that we assume that for each decision time
t\in[2\colon T]
, the statistic
\hat{\beta}_{t-1}^{(n)}
formed by the algorithm converges in probability to a deterministic target policy parameter
\beta_{t}^{*}
as the number of users
n\to\infty
. The parameter
\beta_{t}^{*}
could parameterize a model of the expected reward used by the adaptive sampling algorithm. The target policy parameters
\big\{\beta_{t}^{*}\big\}_{t=1}^{T-1}
, which parameterize the target policies
\big\{\pi_{t}^{*}\big\}_{t=2}^{T}\triangleq\big\{\pi_{t}(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t-1}^{*})\big\}_{t=2}^{T}
.
To allow for a large class of possible target policy parameters, we assume
\beta_{t}^{*}
is the solution to some estimating equation; we formally define these parameters below.

Recall that at the first decision time actions are selected using a pre-specified policy
\pi_{1}
.
For the second decision time, the target policy
\pi_{2}^{*}(\,\cdot\,)\triangleq\pi_{2}(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{1}^{*})
where the target parameter
\beta_{1}^{*}
solves

\bm{0}=\mathbb{E}\left[\phi_{1}\big(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{(i)};\beta_{1}^{*}\big)\right],

for some measurable function
\phi_{1}
of
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{(i)}
indexed by a finite dimensional
\beta_{1}\in\real^{d_{1}}
.

For the third decision time, the target policy
\pi_{3}^{*}(\,\cdot\,)\triangleq\pi_{3}(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{2}^{*})
where the target parameter
\beta_{2}^{*}
solves

\bm{0}=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2}^{*}}\left[\phi_{2}\big(\mathcal{H}_{2}^{(i)};\beta_{2}^{*}\big)\right],

(3.6)

for some measurable function
\phi_{2}
of
\mathcal{H}_{2}^{(i)}
indexed by a finite dimensional
\beta_{2}\in\real^{d_{2}}
.
In display (
3.6
) above, the expectation is indexed by target policies
\pi_{2}^{*}(\,\cdot\,)
. This means that the definition of the target policy for the third decision time,
\pi_{3}^{*}
, depends on the definition of target policy for the second decision time,
\pi_{2}^{*}
.

Continuing this pattern, for the
t^{\textnormal{th}}
decision time, the target policy
\pi_{t}^{*}(\,\cdot\,)\triangleq\pi_{t}(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t-1}^{*})
where the target parameter
\beta_{t}^{*}
solves

\bm{0}=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t-1}^{*}}\left[\phi_{t-1}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(i)};\beta_{t-1}^{*}\big)\right],

(3.7)

for some measurable function
\phi_{t-1}
of
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(i)}
indexed by a finite dimensional
\beta_{t-1}\in\real^{d_{t-1}}
.
Again, the above expectation is indexed by the previous target policies
\pi_{2:t-1}^{*}
.

An example function
\phi_{t-1}
is the following, which corresponds to a least squares solution:

\phi_{t-1}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(i)};\beta_{t-1}\big)=\sum_{t^{\prime}=1}^{t-1}\left(R_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}-\beta_{0,t-1}^{\top}S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}-A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}\beta_{1,t-1}^{\top}S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}\right)\begin{bmatrix}S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}\\
A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}\end{bmatrix}.

(3.8)

In our simulations (Section
6
) we consider a Boltzmann (or Softmax) exploration adaptive sampling algorithm
Asadi and Littman 2017
;
Cesa-Bianchi et al. 2017
;
Sutton and Barto 2018
that forms policies using estimators of the least squares solution
\beta_{t}^{*}
, defined with the estimating function from display (
3.8
).

Similarly, we assume that
\hat{\beta}_{t}^{(n)}
, the estimators of the target policy parameters are Z-estimators, i.e., solutions to the empirical estimating functions. Formally, this means that
\hat{\beta}_{t-1}^{(n)}
satisfies

o_{P}(1/\sqrt{n})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{t-1}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(i)};\hat{\beta}_{t-1}^{(n)}\big)\in\real^{d_{t-1}}.

(3.9)

Remark 4

(Misspecification of the Model used by the Adaptive Algorithm)
.

In general, the adaptive sampling algorithms target parameter
\beta_{t}^{*}
defined in (
3.7
) can parameterize a model for parts of the multivariate distribution of
D^{(i)}
, the user’s underlying potential outcomes from display (
2.1
). We do not require that this model is correct. For example, even if the algorithm is developed assuming the environment corresponds to that of a stochastic contextual bandit, the validity of our statistical analysis will not be affected if this assumption is wrong.

3.2.1
Key Assumptions on Policies

Rather than assume the adaptive sampling algorithm’s model is correctly specified, we instead will make assumptions on the estimators
\big\{\hat{\beta}_{t}^{(n)}\big\}_{t=1}^{T-1}
of the policy parameters and the policy function classes
\left\{\pi_{t}\big(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t-1}\big):\beta_{t-1}\in\real^{d_{t-1}}\right\}
, from display (
3.5
). We now introduce the three foremost assumptions we place on the adaptive sampling policies, Conditions
1
-
3
below (we introduce the other assumptions we place on
\hat{\beta}_{t}^{(n)}
in Section
5.2.1
).

Condition
1
is a consistency condition that ensures that the policy parameter estimator
\hat{\beta}_{t}^{(n)}
formed by the algorithm converges in probability to a target parameter value
\beta_{t}^{*}
as the number of users
n\to\infty
.

Condition 1

(Consistency of Policy Estimators)
.

For each
t\in[1\colon T-1]
,

\hat{\beta}_{t}^{(n)}\overset{P}{\to}\beta_{t}^{*}.

Remark 5

(Sufficient Assumptions for Condition
1
)
.

In Theorem
B.1
of Appendix
B
we state simple sufficient conditions for Condition
1
to hold.

The next two key assumptions we place on the adaptive sampling algorithm, Conditions
2
and
3
, both concern the policy function classes
\left\{\pi_{t}\big(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t-1}\big):\beta_{t-1}\in\real^{d_{t-1}}\right\}
, from display (
3.5
).

Condition 2

(Minimum Exploration)
.

Let
0<\pi_{\min}<1
be a constant.
For all
t\in[2\colon T]
,

\inf_{\beta_{t-1}\in\real^{d_{t-1}}}\pi_{t}\big(a,s;\beta_{t-1}\big)\geq\pi_{\min}

for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
s\in\mathcal{S}
. Also for
t=1
,
\pi_{1}(a,s)\geq\pi_{\min}
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
s\in\mathcal{S}
.

Condition
2
ensures that the policy class produces action selection probabilities that are strictly bounded above zero for all actions.
Note this ensures that the policy is stochastic, as is necessary for micro-randomized trials (discussed earlier in Section
3.1.1
).
Note this condition excludes deterministic policies, which means target policies that maximize the expected reward in classical contextual bandit and Markov decision process environments are excluded. However, in general, the fewer structural assumptions that are placed on the environment, the more need there is for reward-maximizing algorithms to continually explore. For example, in non-stationary and adversarial sequential decision-making problem settings it is common both theoretically and in practice to prevent the RL algorithm’s action selection probabilities to go to zero for any action
Bubeck et al. 2012
;
Cesa-Bianchi and Lugosi 2006
;
Chandak et al. 2020
;
Lattimore and Szepesvári 2020
in order to ensure the algorithm can detect changes in the reward distribution. Action selection probabilities are also commonly constrained away from
0
and
1
to facilitate causal inference and off-policy evaluation after the experiment is over
Hadad et al. 2021
;
Liao et al. 2016
;
Thomas and Brunskill 2016
;
Trella et al. 2022
;
Yao et al. 2021
.

In Condition
3
below, for each
t\in[1\colon T-1]
, we use
B_{t}\subset\real^{d_{t}}
to denote some compact subset whose interior contains
\beta_{t}^{*}
.

Condition 3

(Lipschitz Policy Functions)
.

For all
t\in[2\colon T]
, there is a non-negative, real-valued function
\dot{\pi}_{t}(A_{t}^{(i)},S_{t}^{(i)})
such that
(i)
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\big[\big|\dot{\pi}_{t}(A_{t}^{(i)},S_{t}^{(i)})\big|^{2+\alpha}\big]<\infty
for some
\alpha>0
, and (ii) for any
\beta_{t-1},\beta_{t-1}^{\prime}\in B_{t-1}
,

\big|\pi_{t}\big(A_{t}^{(i)},S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t-1}\big)-\pi_{t}\big(A_{t}^{(i)},S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t-1}\big)\big|\leq\dot{\pi}_{t}(A_{t}^{(i)},S_{t}^{(i)})\big\|\beta_{t-1}-\beta_{t-1}^{\prime}\big\|_{2}~~~\textnormal{a.s.}

Condition
3
is a smoothness condition on the policy function classes, which excludes policies that are a discontinuous function of parameters
\beta_{t-1}
. It is well known in the inference after adaptive sampling literature that standard estimators, like the sample mean, can be asymptotically non-normal on data collected by adaptive algorithms that do not satisfy such smoothness conditions
(
Deshpande et al. 2018
;
Hadad et al. 2021
;
Zhang et al. 2020
)
. Although this smoothness condition may appear rather mild, note that the reward-maximizing policy in a stochastic bandit problem is a discontinuous function of the margin because of the argmax operation; for example, in a two-armed bandit setting with
\beta^{*}\triangleq\mathbb{E}[R_{t}(1)]-\mathbb{E}[R_{t}(0)]
, the optimal policy is
\mathbb{P}(A_{t}=1)=\mathbb{I}_{\beta^{*}>0}
. Despite this, as mentioned after Condition
2
, there are standard reinforcement learning algorithms developed for more complex environments (e.g., non-stationary) which satisfy this smoothness condition.

Remark 6

(Example Algorithms that Satisfy Conditions
2
and
3
)
.

In Appendix
A.2
we show that a Boltzmann (or Softmax) exploration algorithm
Asadi and Littman 2017
;
Cesa-Bianchi et al. 2017
;
Sutton and Barto 2018
and a stochastic mirror descent algorithm (based on those from
(
Lattimore and Szepesvári 2020
, pg 361)
and
Bubeck et al. 2012
) both satisfy Conditions
2
and
3
above.

4
Related Work

Recently, many inference methods have been developed for adaptively sampled data focused on multi-armed and contextual bandit environments. These include inference methods via asymptotic approximations
Bibaut et al. 2021a
;
Chen et al. 2020
;
Deshpande et al. 2018
;
Hadad et al. 2021
;
Zhan et al. 2021
;
Zhang et al. 2020
;
Zhang et al. 2021
as well as approaches that use high probability bounds
Abbasi-Yadkori et al. 2011
;
Brennan et al. 2020
;
Howard et al. 2021
;
Karampatziakis et al. 2021
. These works for the most part consider asymptotics as
T\to\infty
. These methods are more restrictive than ours in that they assume an underlying contextual bandit environment that does not allow a user’s potential outcomes to be dependent over time. Moreover, most of these approaches consider inference for particular estimands, e.g., the value of a policy or a specific treatment effect, rather than an all-purpose Z-estimand. However, these methods are more general than ours in that they put fewer restrictions on the adaptive sampling policies used to collect the batch data, e.g., many allow the action selection probabilities to go to zero at some rate for some actions and do not require their policy function classes to be smooth in its parameters. Additionally, most of these prior methods require that the reward model used by the adaptive sampling algorithm is correctly specified.

The adaptive clinical trial literature provides methods for inference after using policies that satisfy conditions akin to Conditions
2
(Minimum Exploration) and
3
(Lipschitz Policy Functions) above, e.g., see Theorems 3.1 and 9.1 of
Hu and Rosenberger 2006
. There are two ways in which our results differ from these classical results. The first is that we consider a setting in which the adaptive sampling algorithm repeatedly selects treatment actions for each of multiple individuals sequentially over time. Since the adaptive sampling algorithm selects actions probabilistically, each individual is sequentially randomized. In contrast, the adaptive clinical trial literature classically considers settings in which at each decision time a new individual is drawn independently from the population and the adaptive sampling algorithm makes one treatment action decision per individual. The second major difference is that these classical results assume that both the model used for inference and the model used by the adaptive algorithm are correctly specified. For our results, we do not assume either of these models is correctly specified; see Remark
3
for more on model misspecification. Additionally, in Section
5.4
we discuss how our asymptotic results simplify when the estimating function,
\psi
uses a correctly specified model.

Another area of related work is inference methods for longitudinal data. This literature assumes the same underlying potential outcomes model, display (
2.1
), that allows for non-stationarity and dependent outcomes over time within each user
Fitzmaurice et al. 2012
;
Robins 1997
;
Zeger and Liang 1986
. However, this literature considers batch datasets in which user data trajectories are independent across users, which excludes datasets collected by adaptive sampling algorithms that learn across users. This literature also includes methods for inferring excursion effects
Boruvka et al. 2018
;
Qian et al. 2021
, which were discussed in Section
3.1.1
.

Here we generalize techniques from the classical literature on empirical processes for i.i.d. data
Van der Vaart 2000
;
Van Der Vaart and Wellner 1996
to adaptively sampled data. In particular, we develop functional asymptotic normality and maximal inequality results for “Radon-Nikodym derivative weighted empirical processes”; see Section
5.1.2
for more details. Note that
Bibaut et al. 2021b
develops a maximal inequality for adaptively sampled data assuming a classical contextual bandit environment. Besides the differences in the underlying environment assumptions, our maximal inequality results also differ from theirs because they consider asymptotics as
T\to\infty
, while here
T
is fixed and we consider asymptotics as
n\to\infty
.

5
Main Results

Note that if the batch data were collected using the fixed target policies
\pi_{2:T}^{*}
, rather than the data-dependent, adaptive policies
\hat{\pi}_{2:T}^{(n)}
, then the data trajectories would be independent across users, i.e.,
\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)}
would be i.i.d. across
i\in[1\colon n]
. In that i.i.d. setting, we could use standard asymptotic normality results for Z-estimators
(
Van der Vaart 2000
, Theorem 5.21)
to get that
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
is asymptotically normal with the standard sandwich variance, i.e.,

\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\theta}^{(n)}-\theta^{*}\right)\overset{D}{\to}\mathcal{N}\left(\bm{0},~[\dot{\Psi}^{*}]^{-1}\Sigma[\dot{\Psi}^{*}]^{-1,\top}\right),

(5.1)

with “bread”
\dot{\Psi}^{*}\triangleq\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\theta}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\big[\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta\big)\big]\big|_{\theta=\theta^{*}}
and “meat”
\Sigma\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\big[\psi(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta^{*}\big)^{\otimes 2}\big]
; we use the notation
x^{\otimes 2}\triangleq xx^{\top}
.

However, in the adaptively sampled data setting in which the random, data-dependent policies
\hat{\pi}_{2:T}^{(n)}
produced by the adaptive sampling algorithm are used to select actions, we show that the limiting variance is different, specifically,

\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\theta}^{(n)}-\theta^{*}\right)\overset{D}{\to}\mathcal{N}\left(\bm{0},~[\dot{\Psi}^{*}]^{-1}\Sigma^{\mathrm{adapt}}[\dot{\Psi}^{*}]^{-1,\top}\right),

(5.2)

where

\Sigma^{\mathrm{adapt}}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\bigg[\bigg\{\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta^{*}\big)+\dot{\Psi}^{*}\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}M_{t}~\phi_{t}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t}^{*}\big)\bigg\}^{\otimes 2}\bigg].

(5.3)

Above
M_{t}\in\real^{d_{\theta}\times d_{t}}
. See display (
5.32
) for the definition of the
M_{t}
matrices.

We call the limiting variance in display (
5.2
), the
adaptive
sandwich variance. Comparing
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\mathrm{adapt}}
from display (
5.3
), we can interpret the term
\dot{\Psi}^{*}\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}M_{t}~\phi_{t}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t}^{*}\big)
as the “cost” or “inflation” in variance due to using the estimated
\hat{\pi}_{2:T}^{(n)}
to select actions rather than
\pi_{2:T}^{*}
. In special cases, under a property we call “policy invariance”,
M_{t}=0
so the limiting sandwich and adaptive sandwich variances are equal; see Section
5.4
for more details. We provide estimators of the adaptive sandwich variance (see Appendix
A.1.3
), which we use in Section
6
for our simulation results.

We now outline the remainder of this asymptotic results section. In order to provide a high-level understanding of how our proof techniques and results differ from the i.i.d. data case, in Section
5.1
we discuss the ideas behind our asymptotic normality proof; specifically, we introduce the Radon-Nikodym derivative weighting we use in Section
5.1.1
and provide an overview of the functional asymptotic normality results for adaptively sampled data that we develop in Section
5.1.2
.
Then in Section
5.2
, we state our results formally; specifically in Section
5.2.1
we introduce additional assumptions on the policy parameters and in Section
5.2.2
we have our formal theorem statements. In Section
5.3
, we provide a more detailed proof sketch of our main asymptotic normality result, display (
5.2
). Finally, in Section
5.4
, we discuss cases in which the limiting adaptive sandwich variance equals the standard sandwich variance.

5.1
Ideas Behind the Proof of Asymptotic Normality

In order to provide a high-level understanding of how proving results for adaptively sampled data differs from the i.i.d. data case, we now discuss the key ideas that we use in our proof of asymptotic normality. The foremost technical challenge in our proof of asymptotic normality of
\sqrt{n}\big(\hat{\theta}^{(n)}-\theta^{*}\big)
is to account for how the data is collected using estimated policies
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}(\,\cdot\,)=\pi_{t}\big(\,\cdot\,;\hat{\beta}_{t-1}^{(n)}\big)
. Specifically, the challenge is accounting for how the error of estimator
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
is impacted by the error of the estimated policies
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}(\,\cdot\,)=\pi_{t}\big(\,\cdot\,;\hat{\beta}_{t-1}^{(n)}\big)
used to collect the data.

A key insight of this work is that
a Z-estimator
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
formed on adaptively sampled data can be framed as a Z-estimator in which the estimated policy parameters used to collect the data,
\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}
, are plug-in estimates of nuisance parameters.

Classically on i.i.d. data, one constructs Z-estimators with a plug-in estimate of nuisance parameters which are fitted on the same data as the one used to form the Z-estimator itself
Tsiatis 2006
.
In deriving the asymptotic results for the Z-estimator in these classical settings, one must account for the dependence between the Z-estimator of interest and the plug-in estimator because they are constructed using a shared dataset.

However, how to frame the inference after adaptive sampling problem as a problem of inference via a Z-estimator with a plug-in nuisance parameter estimator does not follow straightforwardly from the classical literature. Recall from the definition of
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
from display (
3.2
) that
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
is formed by the data analyzer without constructing any plug-in estimators for nuisance parameters. We are interested in using the methods from the literature on plug-in estimates to account for the error of the policy parameters
\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}
, which impacted how the data was
collected
. On i.i.d. data though, plug-in estimators do not affect
data collection
and are only used for the
data analysis
.

The critical step that will allow us to treat the policy parameters as plug-in nuisance parameters is to write the estimating function for the Z-estimator
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
and the policy parameters
\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}
jointly. The key tool we will use to do this is Radon-Nikodym derivative weighting.

5.1.1
Radon-Nikodym Derivative Weights

Note the following ratios:

\frac{\pi_{t}\big(\,\cdot\,,S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t-1}\big)}{\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}\big(\,\cdot\,,S_{t}^{(i)}\big)}:\mathcal{A}\mapsto[0,\infty).

(5.4)

Conditional on
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(1:n)}
and
S_{t}^{(i)}
, the functions
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}\big(\,\cdot\,,S_{t}^{(i)}\big)
and
\pi_{t}\big(\,\cdot\,,S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t-1}\big)
each define a probability distribution over the action space
\mathcal{A}
. The ratio of these two probability distributions, as seen in display (
5.4
) above, is a Radon-Nikodym derivative; see Lemma
A.3
for a formal statement of this result. Note that in the proof of Lemma
A.3
, we use the minimum exploration Condition
2
(Minimum Exploration), to ensure that these Radon-Nikodym derivatives exist.

For notational convenience, we define the following weighting functions for any
\beta_{t-1},\beta_{t-1}^{\prime}\\
\in\real^{d_{t-1}}
,

W_{t}^{(i)}(\beta_{t-1},\beta_{t-1}^{\prime})~\triangleq~\frac{\pi_{t}\big(A_{t}^{(i)},S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t-1}\big)}{\pi_{t}\big(A_{t}^{(i)},S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t-1}^{\prime}\big)}.

(5.5)

Additionally, for any
\beta_{1:t-1},\beta_{1:t-1}^{\prime}\in\real^{d_{1:t-1}}
(where
d_{1:t-1}\triangleq\sum_{t^{\prime}=1}^{t-1}d_{t^{\prime}}
) we define,

W_{2:t}^{(i)}(\beta_{1:t-1},\beta_{1:t-1}^{\prime})~\triangleq~\prod_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}W_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}(\beta_{t^{\prime}-1},\beta_{t^{\prime}-1}^{\prime}).

The Radon-Nikodym weights above allow us to define estimating functions such as

\Psi\big(\beta_{1:T-1},\theta\big)\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\pi(\beta_{1:T-1})}\left[\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta\big)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[W_{2:T}^{(i)}(\beta_{1:T-1},\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)})\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta\big)\right].

(5.6)

Above we use
\mathbb{E}
to denote expectations with respect to the distribution used to collect the data. Thus, in the expectations from displays (
5.6
) and (
5.8
) above, the Radon-Nikodym weights have the effect of changing the distribution the expectation is taken over. Specifically, it changes the policy with which the actions are selected. Above we use the notation
\mathbb{E}_{\pi(\beta_{1:T-1})}
to denote the expectation with respect to the distribution in which (i) the policies
\big\{\pi_{t}(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t-1})\big\}_{t=2}^{T}
are used to select actions and (ii) user potential outcomes are drawn from
\mathcal{P}
, as described in display (
2.1
).

We also define an empirical version of the limiting estimating function
\Psi\big(\beta_{1:T-1},\theta\big)
above:

\hat{\Psi}^{(n)}\big(\beta_{1:T-1},\theta\big)\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{2:T}^{(i)}(\beta_{1:T-1},\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)})\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta\big).

(5.7)

Additionally, in our proofs we will use the following limiting and empirical estimating functions for the policy parameters:

\Phi_{1:T-1}(\beta_{1:T-1})\triangleq\mathbb{E}\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{(i)};\beta_{1})\\
W_{2}^{(i)}(\beta_{1},\hat{\beta}_{1}^{(n)})\phi_{2}(\mathcal{H}_{2}^{(i)};\beta_{2})\\
W_{2:3}^{(i)}(\beta_{1:2},\hat{\beta}_{1:2}^{(n)})\phi_{3}(\mathcal{H}_{3}^{(i)};\beta_{3})\\
\vdots\\
W_{2:T-1}^{(i)}(\beta_{1:T-2},\hat{\beta}_{1:T-2}^{(n)})\phi_{T-1}(\mathcal{H}_{T-1}^{(i)};\beta_{T-1})\end{bmatrix}

(5.8)

\hat{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}(\beta_{1:T-1})\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{(i)};\beta_{1})\\
W_{2}^{(i)}(\beta_{1},\hat{\beta}_{1}^{(n)})\phi_{2}(\mathcal{H}_{2}^{(i)};\beta_{2})\\
W_{2:3}^{(i)}(\beta_{1:2},\hat{\beta}_{1:2}^{(n)})\phi_{3}(\mathcal{H}_{3}^{(i)};\beta_{3})\\
\vdots\\
W_{2:T-1}^{(i)}(\beta_{1:T-2},\hat{\beta}_{1:T-2}^{(n)})\phi_{T-1}(\mathcal{H}_{T-1}^{(i)};\beta_{T-1})\end{bmatrix}

(5.9)

We use these joint estimating functions for both the policy parameters
\beta_{1:T-1}
and the parameter of interest
\theta
to derive the joint limiting distribution of
\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}
and
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
. Specifically, we prove that
\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}
and
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
are jointly asymptotically normal, as seen in display (
5.10
) below; see Section
5.3
for a proof sketch. Note that since our adaptively sampled data is non-i.i.d. the proof of this result relies heavily on novel functional asymptotic normality results for Radon-Nikodym weighted functions on adaptively sampled data, which we discuss in detail in Section
5.1.2
.

\sqrt{n}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}-\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}\\
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}-\theta^{*}\end{pmatrix}\overset{D}{\to}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\begin{bmatrix}\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}&&\bm{0}\\
\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}&&\dot{\Psi}^{*}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\Sigma_{1:T}\begin{bmatrix}\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}&&\bm{0}\\
\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}&&\dot{\Psi}^{*}\end{bmatrix}^{-1,\top}\right).

(5.10)

Above,

\Sigma_{1:T}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\left[\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{(i)};\beta_{1}^{*})\\
\phi_{2}(\mathcal{H}_{2}^{(i)};\beta_{2}^{*})\\
\vdots\\
\phi_{T-1}(\mathcal{H}_{T-1}^{(i)};\beta_{T-1}^{*})\\
\psi(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta^{*})\end{pmatrix}^{\otimes 2}\right].

(5.11)

and

\begin{bmatrix}\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}&&\bm{0}\\
\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}&&\dot{\Psi}^{*}\end{bmatrix}\triangleq\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\beta_{1:T-1}}}\Phi_{1:T-1}(\beta_{1:T-1})&&\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\theta}}\Phi_{1:T-1}(\beta_{1:T-1})\\
\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\beta_{1:T-1}}}\Psi{}(\beta_{1:T-1},\theta)&&\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\theta}}\Psi{}(\beta_{1:T-1},\theta)\end{bmatrix}\bigg|_{(\beta_{1:T-1},\theta)=(\beta_{1:T-1}^{*},\theta^{*})}.

(5.12)

Note above that
\Phi_{1:T-1}(\beta_{1:T-1}^{*})
is not a function of
\theta
, thus
\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\theta}}\Phi_{1:T-1}(\beta_{1:T-1}^{*})\big|_{\theta=\theta^{*}}=\bm{0}
.

Display (
5.10
) above is sufficient for showing that
\sqrt{n}\big(\hat{\theta}^{(n)}-\theta^{*}\big)
is asymptotically normal with the adaptive sandwich variance from display (
5.1
) holds. Specifically, by Lemma
C.1
(Equivalent Formulations for the Adaptive Sandwich Variance), the lower
d_{\theta}\times d_{\theta}
block of the limiting variance matrix from display (
5.10
) above is equivalent to the adaptive sandwich variance
[\dot{\Psi}^{*}]^{-1}\Sigma^{\mathrm{adapt}}[\dot{\Psi}^{*}]^{-1,\top}
from display (
5.2
).

5.1.2
Overview of Functional Asymptotic Normality Result for Adaptively Sampled Data

The proof of the asymptotic normality result from display (
5.10
) relies heavily on a novel functional asymptotic normality results we develop for adaptively sampled data. Functional asymptotic normality results are classical results from the empirical process literature that are used in many Z-estimator asymptotic normality proofs. These classical results concern stochastic processes of the following form:

\bigg\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})-\mathbb{E}\big[f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})\big]\right)\textnormal{~~s.t.~~}f\in\mathcal{F}\bigg\},

(5.13)

for a class of functions
\mathcal{F}
.
If user data trajectories
\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)}
were i.i.d. over
i\in[1\colon n]
and the complexity of a class of real-valued functions
\mathcal{F}
was properly controlled, then the stochastic process from display (
5.13
) would converge in distribution to a Gaussian process; see Theorem 19.5 of
Van der Vaart 2000
.

For our inference after adaptive sampling problem, we are interested in showing a functional asymptotic normality result for stochastic processes like the following:

\left\{\sqrt{n}\big[\hat{\Psi}^{(n)}(\beta_{1:T-1},\theta)-\Psi(\beta_{1:T-1},\theta)\big]\textnormal{~~s.t.~~}\beta_{1:T-1}\in B_{1:T-1},\theta\in\Theta\right\}\\
=\bigg\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(W_{2:T}^{(i)}(\beta_{1:T-1},\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)})\psi(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta)-\mathbb{E}\left[W_{2:T}^{(i)}(\beta_{1:T-1},\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)})\psi(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta)\right]\right)\\
\textnormal{~~s.t.~~}\beta_{1:T-1}\in B_{1:T-1},~\theta\in\Theta\bigg\}.

(5.14)

Above
B_{1:T-1}\subset\real^{d_{1:T-1}}
and
\Theta\subset\real^{d_{\theta}}
are compact balls whose interiors contain
\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}
and
\theta^{*}
respectively.
Also, recall that above we use the expectation
\mathbb{E}
(not indexed by any policies) to refer to the distribution which was used to generate the data. Note that this means that
\mathbb{E}\left[W_{2:T}^{(i)}(\beta_{1:T-1},\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)})\psi(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi(\beta_{1:T-1})}\left[\psi(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta)\right]
.

Note that to show that the stochastic process from display (
5.14
) is functionally asymptotically normal, it is sufficient to show a functional asymptotic normality result for empirical processes of the form

\bigg\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\big\{\hat{\pi}_{2:T}^{(i)}\big\}^{-1}f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})-\mathbb{E}\left[\big\{\hat{\pi}_{2:T}^{(i)}\big\}^{-1}f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})\right]\right)\textnormal{~~s.t.~~}f\in\mathcal{F}\bigg\},

(5.15)

where
\hat{\pi}^{(i)}_{2:T}\triangleq\prod_{t=2}^{T}\hat{\pi}_{t}\big(A_{t}^{(i)},S_{t}^{(i)}\big)
.
Specifically, the stochastic process from display (
5.14
) is equivalent to the stochastic process in display (
5.15
) for the following choice of
\mathcal{F}
:

\mathcal{F}=\bigg\{\bigg[\prod_{t=2}^{t}\pi_{t}(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t-1})\bigg]\psi(\,\cdot\,;\theta)\textnormal{~~s.t.~~}\beta_{1:T-1}\in B_{1:T-1},\theta\in\Theta\bigg\}.

By Theorem 18.14 of
Van der Vaart 2000
, the two conditions needed to ensure a functional normality result for display (
5.15
) are (i) a joint asymptotic normality result for the stochastic process evaluated at any finite number of functions
f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{k}\in\mathcal{F}
, and (ii) a maximal inequality result over the function class
\mathcal{F}
. To show part (i) holds for adaptively sampled data, we prove a Weighted Martingale Triangular Array Central Limit Theorem (Theorem
D.4
). Specifically this Theorem can be used to show asymptotic normality results like the following:

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\big\{\hat{\pi}_{2:T}^{(i)}\big\}^{-1}f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})-\mathbb{E}\left[\big\{\hat{\pi}_{2:T}^{(i)}\big\}^{-1}f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})\right]\right)\\
\overset{D}{\to}\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,~\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\left[\big\{\pi_{2:T}^{*,(i)}\big\}^{-2}f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\left[\big\{\pi_{2:T}^{*,(i)}\big\}^{-1}f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})\right]^{2}\bigg).

Above
\pi^{*,(i)}_{2:T}\triangleq\prod_{t=2}^{T}\pi_{t}^{*}\big(A_{t}^{(i)},S_{t}^{(i)}\big)
. The proof of our asymptotic normality result heavily relies on Lipschitz policy function Condition
3
and builds on the martingale Central Limit Theorem from Theorem 2.2 of
Dvoretzky 1972
.

For part (ii), we prove a maximal inequality for adaptively sampled data as a function of the bracketing integral of
\mathcal{F}
, Lemma
E.4
. We prove our maximal inequality, Lemma
E.4
, using a novel Weighted Martingale Bernstein Inequality, Lemma
E.2
. This inequality modifies the classical Bernstein inequality for i.i.d. data
(
Van der Vaart 2000
, Lemma 19.32)
.
Specifically, our Bernstein inequality ensures that on our adaptively sampled data type, for any real-valued function
f
of
\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)}
with
\|f\|_{\infty}<\infty
,

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\bigg|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\hat{\pi}_{2:T}^{(i)}\big\}^{-1}f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})-\mathbb{E}\left[\big\{\hat{\pi}_{2:T}^{(i)}\big\}^{-1}f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})\right]\bigg|>x\bigg)\\
\leq 2\exp\bigg(-\frac{\pi_{\min}^{T-1}}{4}\frac{x^{2}}{\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\big[\big\{\pi_{2:T}^{*,(i)}\big\}^{-1}f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})^{2}\big]+x\|f\|_{\infty}/\sqrt{n}}\bigg),

(5.16)

for any
x>0
and
n\geq 1
.

We now discuss the key techniques used in the proof of Lemma
E.2
.
Our proof of Lemma
E.2
, similar to the classical Bernstein inequality proof, starts by using a Chernoff bound to get an upper tail bound. Specifically, for any
\lambda>0
,

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\big\{\hat{\pi}_{2:T}^{(i)}\big\}^{-1}f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})-\mathbb{E}\left[\big\{\hat{\pi}_{2:T}^{(i)}\big\}^{-1}f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})\right]\right)>x\bigg)

\leq e^{-\lambda x}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\exp\bigg\{\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigg(\big\{\hat{\pi}_{2:T}^{(i)}\big\}^{-1}f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})-\mathbb{E}\left[\big\{\hat{\pi}_{2:T}^{(i)}\big\}^{-1}f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})\right]\bigg)\bigg\}\bigg].

Changing the summation in exponent into a product,

=e^{-\lambda x}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\exp\bigg\{\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg(\big\{\hat{\pi}_{2:T}^{(i)}\big\}^{-1}f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})-\mathbb{E}\left[\big\{\hat{\pi}_{2:T}^{(i)}\big\}^{-1}f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})\right]\bigg)\bigg\}\bigg].

In the original proof for i.i.d. data, the next step is to move the product over
i=1,2,\dots n
above outside of the expectation. If we omit the terms
\big\{\hat{\pi}_{2:T}^{(i)}\big\}^{-1}
above and if user trajectories
\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)}
were i.i.d. over
i\in[1\colon n]
, moving the product outside the expectation would be trivial. However, this is not the case for our adaptively sampled data setting.

The key insight we use in our proof is Lemma
E.1
, a result that allows us to move products out of expectations using the
\big\{\hat{\pi}_{2:T}^{(i)}\big\}^{-1}
weighting. Specifically, this Lemma proves that for any constant
c
,

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\big\{\hat{\pi}_{2:T}^{(i)}\big\}^{-1}f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})+c\right)\bigg]=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\bigg[\big\{\pi_{2:T}^{*,(i)}\big\}^{-1}f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})+c\bigg].

The proof leverages the conditional independence of the action selection at each time step and the fact that the underlying potential outcomes are i.i.d.
See Appendix
E
for more details on all our maximal inequality results.

5.2
Formal Statement of Results

We now formally state the additional conditions we use to show consistency and asymptotic normality of
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
. Below we first provide assumptions on the estimated policy parameters
\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}
(Section
5.2.1
) and then provide the theorem for, and assumptions on, the estimator
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
based on the resulting adaptively sampled data (Section
5.2.2
). The rationale for this is first, separating out the conditions on the inferential estimator
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
will also make explicit the conditions placed on the
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
Z-estimator due to the adaptive sampling. A second consideration is that designers of adaptive sampling algorithms will know what assumptions on the algorithm are sufficient so that the resulting data can be used in a wide variety of after-study data analyses.
A third consideration is that a data analyst who is provided an adaptively sampled dataset (with a known algorithm) can devise tests on the data to challenge the assumptions made on the policy parameters
\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}
.

5.2.1
Assumptions on the Policy Parameters

We now discuss formally the remaining assumptions we place on the policy parameter estimators
\hat{\beta}_{t}^{(n)}
and their estimating functions
\phi_{t}
. (Recall the assumption that estimators
\hat{\beta}_{t}^{(n)}
are consistent for
\beta_{t}^{*}
and the assumptions placed on the policy function classes
\left\{\pi_{t}\big(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t-1}\big):\beta_{t-1}\in\real^{d_{t-1}}\right\}
were introduced earlier in Section
3.2
.) The first of these assumptions, Condition
4
below, will use the notation
\mathbb{E}_{\pi(\beta_{1:T-1})}
to denote the expectation with respect to the distribution in which (i) the policies
\big\{\pi_{t}(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t-1})\big\}_{t=2}^{T}
are used to select actions and (ii) user potential outcomes are drawn from
\mathcal{P}
, as described in display (
2.1
).

Condition 4

(Differentiability of Policy Parameter Estimating Functions)
.

The following mapping is differentiable at
\beta_{1:T-1}=\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}

\beta_{1:T-1}\mapsto\Phi_{1:T-1}(\beta_{1:T-1})\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\pi(\beta_{1:T-1})}\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{(i)};\beta_{1})\\
\phi_{2}(\mathcal{H}_{2}^{(i)};\beta_{2})\\
\vdots\\
\phi_{T-1}(\mathcal{H}_{T-1}^{(i)};\beta_{T-1})\end{bmatrix}.

(5.17)

Above the function
\Phi_{1:T-1}(\beta_{1:T-1})
was first defined in display (
5.17
).
We also assume that for each
t\in[1\colon T-1]
, the derivative matrix
\dot{\Phi}_{t}^{*}\triangleq\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\beta_{t}}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\big[\phi_{t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t})\big]\big|_{\beta_{t}=\beta_{t}^{*}}
is invertible.

Note in the expectation above in display (
5.17
) that
\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)}
only depends on the policies used to select actions up to decision time
t
, i.e., policies
\big\{\pi_{t^{\prime}}(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t^{\prime}-1})\big\}_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}
. Thus,
\mathbb{E}_{\pi(\beta_{1:T-1})}\big[\phi_{t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t})\big]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi(\beta_{1:t-1})}\big[\phi_{t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t})\big]
.

Condition
4
ensures that the estimating functions for the policy parameters
\beta_{1:T-1}
are differentiable at
\beta_{1:T-1}=\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}
. This kind of differentiability condition is common for Z-estimators
(
Van der Vaart 2000
, Theorem 5.21)
.
What is notable about Condition
4
is that in display (
5.17
), the policy parameters
\beta_{1:T-1}
parameterize not only the estimating functions
\phi_{t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t})
, they also parameterize the distribution with which the expectation is taken,
\mathbb{E}_{\pi(\beta_{1:T-1})}
.

The final assumption we place on the policies, Condition
5
, is a Lipschitz condition on the policy parameter estimating functions
\phi_{t}
. This condition has the effect of restricting the complexity of the function class
\big\{\phi_{t}(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t}):\beta_{t}\in B_{t}\big\}
and has been used in other standard proofs for the asymptotic normality of Z-estimators, e.g., see Theorem 5.21 of
Van der Vaart 2000
.
Below, for each
t\in[1\colon T-1]
, we use
B_{t}\subset\real^{d_{t}}
to denote some compact subset whose interior contains
\beta_{t}^{*}
.

Condition 5

(Lipschitz Policy Estimating Function)
.

Let
\alpha>0
be a constant. For each
t\in[2\colon T]
, there is a non-negative valued function
\dot{\phi}_{t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})
such that

(i)

For any
\beta_{t},\beta_{t}^{\prime}\in B_{t}
,

\big\|\phi_{t}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t}\big)-\phi_{t}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t}^{\prime}\big)\big\|_{2}\leq\dot{\phi}_{t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\big\|\beta_{t}-\beta_{t}^{\prime}\big\|_{2}~~~\textnormal{a.s.}

(5.18)

(ii)

\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\big[\big|\dot{\phi}_{t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\big|^{2+\alpha}\big]<\infty
.

(iii)

\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\big[\big|\dot{\phi}_{t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\dot{\pi}_{t^{\prime}}(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)})\big|^{2+\alpha}\big]<\infty
for all
t^{\prime}\in[2\colon t]
(the function
\dot{\pi}_{t^{\prime}}
is from Condition
3
).

Additionally, for each
t\in[2\colon T]
, let

(iv)

\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\left[\big\|\phi_{t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t}^{*})\big\|_{2}^{2+\alpha}\right]<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\left[\big\|\phi_{t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t}^{*})\dot{\pi}_{t^{\prime}}(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)})\big\|_{2}^{2+\alpha}\right]<\infty
for all
t^{\prime}\in[2\colon t]
.

In Condition
5
parts
(ii)
and
(iv)
above, we assume finite moment conditions that involve the functions
\dot{\pi}_{t^{\prime}}
from Condition
3
. This will allow us to control the complexity of the function classes
\big\{\big[\prod_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\pi_{t^{\prime}}(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t^{\prime}-1})\big]\phi_{t}(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t})\textnormal{~~s.t.~~}\beta_{1:t}\in B_{1:t}\big\}
for
t\in[2\colon T-1]
; see the Remark below Theorem
B.3
in Appendix
B.3
for more details. Note that these assumptions involving
\dot{\pi}_{t^{\prime}}
in Condition
5
are relatively mild and are satisfied if
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\big[\big|\dot{\pi}_{t}(A_{t}^{(i)},S_{t}^{(i)})\big|^{4+2\alpha}\big]<\infty
for all
t\in[2\colon T]
, and
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\big[\big|\dot{\phi}_{t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\big|^{4+2\alpha}\big]<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\left[\big\|\phi_{t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t}^{*})\big\|_{2}^{4+2\alpha}\right]<\infty
for all
t\in[1\colon T-1]
.

Remark 7

(More General Policy Estimating Functions)
.

We use Condition
5
to help ensure a stochastic equicontinuity result holds for the policy parameters. Condition
5
can be replaced by more general conditions involving bracketing numbers for function classes
\big\{\phi_{t}(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t})\textnormal{~~s.t.~~}\beta_{t}\in B_{t}\big\}
for
t\in[1\colon T-1]
. See Appendix
B.3
for the statement of the stochastic equicontinuity result and the more general sufficient conditions.

5.2.2
Theorem Statements

We have two main theorems. The first, Theorem
5.1
, shows the consistency of
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
, i.e., that
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}\overset{P}{\to}\theta^{*}
.
The second, Theorem
5.2
, shows that
\sqrt{n}\big(\hat{\theta}^{(n)}-\theta^{*}\big)
is asymptotically normal with the adaptive sandwich limiting variance from display (
5.2
). Conditions
1
-
5
introduced earlier in Sections
3.2.1
and
5.2.1
are all the assumptions we make on the adaptive sampling algorithm for these two theorems. The remaining assumptions will concern the Z-estimation function
\psi
(used to define the inferential target
\theta^{*}
and estimator
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
). We will require that
\psi
“plays nicely” with the adaptive sampling algorithm. In other words, there may be choices of
\psi
that make it incompatible with the adaptive sampling algorithm used to collect the data.

We now state our main theorems. In the conditions for these theorems, we use bracketing numbers to control the complexity of function classes. The bracketing number of a class of real-valued functions
\mathcal{F}
is the number of brackets, i.e., pairs functions, of a certain “size” needed to cover
\mathcal{F}
. Following the notation used in Chapter 19 of
Van der Vaart 2000
, for any function class
\mathcal{F}
of real-valued, measurable functions of
\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)}
, we use
N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)
to denote the number of brackets of size
\epsilon
in
L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})
norm needed to cover
\mathcal{F}
; see Appendix
A.4.1
for a formal definition of bracketing numbers.

Theorem 5.1

(Consistency)
.

We assume that Conditions
1
-
3
hold for the adaptive sampling algorithm.
Then

\hat{\theta}^{(n)}\overset{P}{\to}\theta^{*}

under the following assumptions on the estimator
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
and its corresponding estimating function
\psi
:

(C1)

Well-Separated Solution:

For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists some
\eta>0
such that

\inf_{\theta\in\real^{d_{\theta}}\textnormal{~s.t.~}\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{1}>\epsilon}\left\|\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\big[\psi(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta)\big]\right\|_{1}>\eta>0.

(C2)

Asymptotically Tight:

For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists some
k<\infty
such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\big(\big\|\hat{\theta}^{(n)}\big\|_{1}>k\big)\leq\epsilon.

(C3)

Finite Bracketing Number:

Let
\alpha>0
be a constant. For any compact subset
K_{\theta}\subset\real^{d_{\theta}}
,

(i)

For any
\epsilon>0
and any vector
c\in\real^{d_{\theta}}
, the bracketing number

N_{[~]}\left(\epsilon,~\big\{c^{\top}\psi(\,\cdot\,;\theta)\textnormal{~~s.t.~~}\theta\in K_{\theta}\big\},~L_{1+\alpha}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\right)<\infty.

(5.19)

(ii)

There exists a function
F_{\psi}
where for any
\theta\in K_{\theta}
,
\big\|\psi(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta)\big\|_{1}\leq F_{\psi}(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})
a.s. and

\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\left[\big|F_{\psi}(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})\dot{\pi}_{t}(A_{t}^{(i)},S_{t}^{(i)})\big|^{1+\alpha}\right]<\infty

(5.20)

for all
t\in[2\colon T]
; the functions
\dot{\pi}_{t}
above are from Condition
3
.

Assumption
(C1)
is used to ensure that there is a well-separated solution for the inferential quantity of interest,
\theta^{*}
; this also ensures that
\theta^{*}
is a unique root of the Z-estimation criterion from display (
3.1
). This well-separated condition is commonly used in consistency proofs for Z-estimators
(
Van der Vaart 2000
, Theorem 5.9)
.

Next, assumption
(C2)
is used to ensure that the estimator
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
is asymptotically tight, i.e., does not tend towards infinity. In general, this assumption holds when the
\theta
parameter space is bounded or when the estimating function
\psi
is the derivative of a concave function, e.g., see Theorem 5.14 of
Van der Vaart 2000
.

Finally, assumption
(C3)
restricts the complexity of the function classes

\big\{c^{\top}\psi(\,\cdot\,;\theta)\textnormal{~~s.t.~~}\theta\in K_{\theta}\big\}
via bracketing numbers. For i.i.d. data, finite bracketing number conditions akin to display (
5.19
) of assumption
(C3)
are used to show uniform law of large number results
(
Van Der Vaart and Wellner 1996
, Theorem 19.4)
. However, since the adaptively sampled data is not i.i.d., we use assumption
(C3)
to prove a martingale version of a uniform law of large numbers (Theorem
D.2
). Specifically, our martingale uniform law of large numbers will concern functions weighted by Radon-Nikodym derivatives. To facilitate use of these Radon-Nikodym weights we control the bracketing complexity of the function class

\mathcal{F}_{\Pi c^{\top}\psi}\big(B_{1:T-1},K_{\theta})\triangleq\bigg\{\bigg[\prod_{t=2}^{T}\pi_{t}(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t-1})\bigg]c^{\top}\psi(\,\cdot\,;\theta\big)\textnormal{~~s.t.~~}\beta_{1:T-1}\in B_{1:T-1},\theta\in K_{\theta}\bigg\}.

(5.21)

In particular, we use display (
5.20
) of assumption
(C3)
to help ensure that for any
\epsilon>0
and any
c\in\real^{d_{\theta}}
,
N_{[~]}\left(\epsilon,~\mathcal{F}_{\Pi c^{\top}\psi}(B_{1:T-1},K_{\theta}),~L_{1+\alpha}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\right)<\infty
; see Lemma
A.4
.

Theorem 5.2

(Asymptotic Normality)
.

We assume that Conditions
1
-
5
hold for the adaptive sampling algorithm.
Furthermore, we assume that
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}\overset{P}{\to}\theta^{*}
holds (result of Theorem
5.1
). Then, for
\dot{\Psi}^{*}\triangleq\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\theta}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\big[\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta\big)\big]\big|_{\theta=\theta^{*}}
and for
\Sigma^{\mathrm{adapt}}
as defined in display (
5.3
),

\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\theta}^{(n)}-\theta^{*}\right)\overset{D}{\to}\mathcal{N}\left(\bm{0},~[\dot{\Psi}^{*}]^{-1}\Sigma^{\mathrm{adapt}}[\dot{\Psi}^{*}]^{-1,\top}\right),

under the following additional assumptions:

(N1)

Invertible “Bread”:

The mapping
\theta\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\big[\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta\big)\big]
is differentiable at
\theta=\theta^{*}
. Moreover, the derivative matrix
\dot{\Psi}^{*}\triangleq\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\theta}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\big[\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta\big)\big]\big|_{\theta=\theta^{*}}
is invertible.

(N2)

Differentiable with Respect to Policy Parameters:

The mapping
\beta_{1:T-1}\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}(\beta_{1:T-1})}\big[\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta^{*}\big)\big]
is differentiable at
\beta_{1:T-1}=\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}
.

(N3)

Continuity Condition:

The following mapping is continuous at
\theta=\theta^{*}
:

\theta\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\left[\big\|\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta\big)-\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta^{*}\big)\big\|_{2}^{2}\right].

(N4)

Finite Bracketing Integral:

Let
\Theta\subset\real^{d_{\theta}}
be a compact subset whose interior contains
\theta^{*}
and let
\alpha>0
be a constant.

(i)

For any vector
c\in\real^{d_{\theta}}
,

\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log N_{[~]}\left(\epsilon,~\big\{c^{\top}\psi(\,\cdot\,;\theta)\textnormal{~~s.t.~~}\theta\in\Theta\big\},~L_{2+\alpha}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\right)}d\epsilon<\infty.

(5.22)

(ii)

There exists a function
F_{\psi}
such that for all
\theta\in\Theta
,
\big\|\psi(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta)\big\|_{1}\leq F_{\psi}(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})
a.s. and

\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\left[\big|F_{\psi}(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})\dot{\pi}_{t}(A_{t}^{(i)},S_{t}^{(i)})\big|^{2+\alpha}\right]<\infty

(5.23)

for all
t\in[2\colon T]
; the functions
\dot{\pi}_{t}
above are from Condition
3
.

We now discuss each of the four parts of the assumptions of Theorem
5.2
(Asymptotic Normality). Assumption
(N1)
is used to ensure the “bread” part of the adaptive sandwich variance,
\dot{\Psi}^{*}
, is invertible. Note that the standard sandwich variance for Z-estimators on i.i.d. data also requires this condition to hold; see the discussion above display (
5.1
).

Assumption
(N2)
ensures that the estimating function
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}(\beta_{1:T-1})}\big[\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta^{*}\big)\big]
is differentiable with respect to the policy parameters
\beta_{1:T-1}
. This condition is specific to the adaptively sampled data setting. The derivative
\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\beta_{1:T-1}}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}(\beta_{1:T-1})}\big[\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta^{*}\big)\big]\big|_{\beta_{1:T-1}=\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}}
is a component of the terms
M_{t}
from
\Sigma^{\mathrm{adapt}}
in the adaptive sandwich variance; see display (
5.3
) for the definition of
\Sigma^{\mathrm{adapt}}
and see display (
5.32
) for the definition of
M_{t}
.

Assumption
(N3)
is used to ensure that if
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}\overset{P}{\to}\theta^{*}
, then the
L_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})
distance between
\psi\big(\,\cdot\,;\hat{\theta}^{(n)}\big)
and
\psi\big(\,\cdot\,;\theta^{*}\big)
converges in probability to zero. This type of condition is classically used in the empirical processes literature to show stochastic equicontinuity results
(
Van der Vaart 2000
, Lemma 19.24)
.

Finally, display (
5.22
) of assumption
(N4)
is a finite bracketing integral condition on the function class
\big\{c^{\top}\psi(\,\cdot\,;\theta)\textnormal{~~s.t.~~}\theta\in\Theta\big\}
for any
c\in\real^{d_{\theta}}
. Note that if this function class is Lipschitz, this is sufficient for the finite bracketing integral condition in display (
5.22
) to hold
(
Van der Vaart 2000
, Example 19.7)
. For i.i.d. data, this kind of bracketing integral condition is used to show functional asymptotic normality results
(
Van der Vaart 2000
, Theorem 19.5)
. Similarly, we show a functional asymptotic normality result for adaptively sampled data using display (
5.22
); this was discussed in more detail earlier in Section
5.1.2
. Our central limit theorem considers functions weighted by particular Radon-Nikodym derivatives. Due to our use of these Radon-Nikodym weights, we will need to control the bracketing integral of the function class
\mathcal{F}_{\Pi c^{\top}\psi}\big(B_{1:T-1},\Theta)
as defined earlier in display (
5.21
). In particular, we use display (
5.23
) of assumption
(N4)
to help ensure that for any
c\in\real^{d_{\theta}}
,
\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log N_{[~]}\left(\epsilon,~\mathcal{F}_{\Pi c^{\top}\psi}(B_{1:T-1},\Theta),~L_{2+\alpha}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\right)}d\epsilon<\infty
; see Lemma
A.4
.

5.3
Proof Sketch for Theorem
5.2
(Asymptotic Normality)

As first mentioned below display (
5.10
), by Lemma
C.1
(Equivalent Formulations for the Adaptive Sandwich Variance), the following result is sufficient for the Theorem and will be the main result we show in this proof:

\sqrt{n}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}-\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}\\
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}-\theta^{*}\end{pmatrix}\overset{D}{\to}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\begin{bmatrix}\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}&&\bm{0}\\
\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}&&\dot{\Psi}^{*}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\Sigma_{1:T}\begin{bmatrix}\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}&&\bm{0}\\
\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}&&\dot{\Psi}^{*}\end{bmatrix}^{-1,\top}\right).

(5.24)

The derivative terms
\dot{\Psi}^{*}
,
\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}
, and
\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}
above exist by assumptions
(N1)
,
(N2)
, and Condition
4
respectively.

We now state several equalities and discuss why they hold below:

-\sqrt{n}\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}\big(\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}\big)-\Phi_{1:T-1}\big(\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}\big)\\
\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\smash{\underbrace{\phantom{\begin{matrix}\hat{\Psi}^{(n)}\big(\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)},\hat{\theta}^{(n)}\big)\end{matrix}}}_{\text{$=o_{P}(1/\sqrt{n})$}}}$}\hat{\Psi}^{(n)}\big(\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)},\hat{\theta}^{(n)}\big)-\Psi\big(\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)},\hat{\theta}^{(n)}\big)\end{bmatrix}\\
~~\\
\underbrace{=}_{(a)}-\sqrt{n}\begin{bmatrix}\Phi_{1:T-1}\big(\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}\big)-\Phi_{1:T-1}\big(\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}\big)\\
\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\smash{\underbrace{\phantom{\begin{matrix}\Psi\big(\beta_{1:T-1}^{*},\theta^{*}\big)\end{matrix}}}_{\text{$=0$}}}$}\Psi\big(\beta_{1:T-1}^{*},\theta^{*}\big)-\Psi\big(\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)},\hat{\theta}^{(n)}\big)\end{bmatrix}+o_{P}(1)\\
~~\\
\underbrace{=}_{(b)}\sqrt{n}\begin{bmatrix}\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}&&\bm{0}\\
\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}&&\dot{\Psi}^{*}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}-\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}\\
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}-\theta^{*}\end{bmatrix}+\sqrt{n}o_{P}\left(\left\|\begin{matrix}\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}-\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}\\
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}-\theta^{*}\end{matrix}\right\|_{2}\right)+o_{P}(1).

(5.25)

Equality (a) above holds since
\hat{\Psi}^{(n)}\big(\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)},\hat{\theta}^{(n)}\big)=o_{P}(1/\sqrt{n})
and
\Psi\big(\beta_{1:T-1}^{*},\theta^{*}\big)=0
by the definitions of
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
and
\theta^{*}
from displays (
3.2
) and (
3.1
) respectively;
also since
\hat{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}\big(\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}\big)=o_{P}(1/\sqrt{n})
and
\Phi_{1:T-1}\big(\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}\big)=0
by the definitions of
\hat{\beta}_{t}^{(n)}
and
\beta_{t}^{*}
from displays (
3.9
) and (
3.7
).

Equality (b) above holds by a Taylor series expansion. Specifically by assumptions
(N1)
and
(N2)
, the mapping
\big(\beta_{1:T-1},\theta\big)\mapsto\Psi(\beta_{1:T-1},\theta)
is differentiable at
\big(\beta_{1:T-1},\theta\big)=\big(\beta_{1:T-1}^{*},\theta^{*}\big)
. Also the mapping
\beta_{1:T-1}\mapsto\Phi_{1:T-1}(\beta_{1:T-1})
is differentiable at
\beta_{1:T-1}=\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}
by Condition
4
. As mentioned below display (
5.12
), since
\Phi_{1:T-1}(\beta_{1:T-1}^{*})
is not a function of
\theta
,
\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\theta}}\Phi_{1:T-1}(\beta_{1:T-1}^{*})\big|_{\theta=\theta^{*}}=\bm{0}
.

We now state the next set of results and discuss why they hold below:

-\sqrt{n}\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}\big(\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}\big)-\Phi_{1:T-1}\big(\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}\big)\\
\hat{\Psi}^{(n)}\big(\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)},\hat{\theta}^{(n)}\big)-\Psi\big(\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)},\hat{\theta}^{(n)}\big)\end{bmatrix}\\
\underbrace{=}_{(c)}-\sqrt{n}\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}\big(\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}\big)-\Phi_{1:T-1}\big(\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}\big)\\
\hat{\Psi}^{(n)}\big(\beta_{1:T-1}^{*},\theta^{*}\big)-\Psi\big(\beta_{1:T-1}^{*},\theta^{*}\big)\end{bmatrix}+o_{P}(1)\underbrace{\overset{D}{\to}}_{(d)}\mathcal{N}\big(0,\Sigma_{1:T}\big).

(5.26)

Equality (c) above is an stochastic equicontinuity result that holds by applying Lemma
D.6
(Stochastic Equicontinuity). Lemma
D.6
uses the fact that
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}\overset{P}{\to}\theta^{*}
(by assumption of the Theorem) and
\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}\overset{P}{\to}\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}
(by Condition
1
). Ensuring that the other assumptions needed to apply Lemma
D.6
are satisfied is more involved; see the proofs of Theorems
B.3
and
5.2
for more details. The proof of Lemma
D.6
relies on a functional asymptotic normality result that we prove holds on adaptively sampled data for functions weighted by the Radon-Nikodym derivatives (see Section
5.1.2
).

Asymptotic normality result (d) above holds by Theorem
D.4
(Weighted Martingale Triangular Array Central Limit Theorem). This Theorem proves a martingale central limit theorem for functions weighted by the Radon-Nikodym derivatives on adaptively sampled data.

By consolidating the results from displays (
5.25
) and (
5.26
) above, and applying Slutsky’s theorem we get the following result:

\sqrt{n}\begin{bmatrix}\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}&&\bm{0}\\
\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}&&\dot{\Psi}^{*}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}-\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}\\
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}-\theta^{*}\end{bmatrix}+\sqrt{n}o_{P}\left(\left\|\begin{matrix}\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}-\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}\\
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}-\theta^{*}\end{matrix}\right\|_{2}\right)\overset{D}{\to}\mathcal{N}\big(0,\Sigma_{1:T}\big).

Note that
\dot{\Psi}^{*}
is invertible by assumption
(N1)
and
\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}
is invertible by Condition
4
and Lemma
B.4
. By Proposition
1
(Blockwise Inversion of Matrices), this is sufficient for
\begin{bmatrix}\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}&&\bm{0}\\
\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}&&\dot{\Psi}^{*}\end{bmatrix}
to be invertible.
Thus, by the continuous mapping theorem,

\sqrt{n}\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}-\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}\\
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}-\theta^{*}\end{bmatrix}+\sqrt{n}O(1)o_{P}\left(\left\|\begin{matrix}\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}-\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}\\
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}-\theta^{*}\end{matrix}\right\|_{2}\right)\\
\overset{D}{\to}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\begin{bmatrix}\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}&&\bm{0}\\
\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}&&\dot{\Psi}^{*}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\Sigma_{1:T}\begin{bmatrix}\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}&&\bm{0}\\
\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}&&\dot{\Psi}^{*}\end{bmatrix}^{-1,\top}\right).

The above implies that
\sqrt{n}\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}-\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}\\
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}-\theta^{*}\end{bmatrix}=O_{P}(1)
. This means that

\sqrt{n}O(1)o_{P}\left(\left\|\begin{matrix}\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}-\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}\\
