\hat{\theta}^{(n)}-\theta^{*}\end{matrix}\right\|_{2}\right)=o_{P}(1)
.
Thus, display (
5.24
) holds by Slutky’s Theorem.

5.4
Cases in which the Adaptive and Standard Sandwich Variances are Equal

In this section, we now discuss formally the conditions under which the adaptive sandwich variance equals the standard sandwich variance. Specifically, we show that this equivalence holds under a property of estimands
\theta^{*}
, which we call
policy invariance
.

Definition 5.1

(Policy Invariance)
.

We say that
\theta^{*}
is policy invariant if

0=\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}\triangleq\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\beta_{1:T-1}}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi(\beta_{1:T-1})}\left[\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta\big)\right]\bigg|_{\beta_{1:T-1}=\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}}.

(5.27)

We use also use the notation
\big[V_{T,1},V_{T,2},\dots,V_{T,T-1}\big]\triangleq\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}\in\real^{d_{\theta}\times d_{1:T-1}}
.

Using the Radon-Nikodym derivative weighting from (
5.5
) we can equivalently write
V_{T,t}
as follows:

V_{T,t}\triangleq\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\beta_{t}}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\left[W_{t+1}^{(i)}(\beta_{t},\beta_{t}^{*})\psi(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta^{*})\right]\bigg|_{\beta_{t}=\beta_{t}^{*}}\in\real^{d_{\theta}\times d_{t}}.

(5.28)

We can interpret
V_{T,t}
as how the estimating function for
\theta^{*}
changes with small changes in the limiting policy parameter
\beta_{t}^{*}
. A particular case in which
\theta^{*}
is policy invariant was first discussed informally in Remark
3
; specifically when
\psi
is chosen to be a derivative of the likelihood function of a model for a particular outcome, if that model is correctly specified then the estimand
\theta^{*}
will not be a projection and will not depend on the target policy parameters
\beta_{1:T-1}^{*}
.

Recall from display (
5.3
) that the adaptive sandwich variance is
[\dot{\Psi}^{*}]^{-1}\Sigma^{\mathrm{adapt}}[\dot{\Psi}^{*}]^{-1}
where

\Sigma^{\mathrm{adapt}}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\bigg[\bigg\{\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta^{*}\big)+\dot{\Psi}^{*}\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}M_{t}~\phi_{t}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t}^{*}\big)\bigg\}^{\otimes 2}\bigg].

(5.29)

We now discuss how the policy invariance property will ensure that the sandwich and adaptive sandwich variances are equivalent.

Recall from the proof sketch from Section
5.3
that by Lemma
C.1
(Equivalent Formulations for the Adaptive Sandwich Variance),
[\dot{\Psi}^{*}]^{-1}\Sigma^{\mathrm{adapt}}[\dot{\Psi}^{*}]^{-1}
, the adaptive sandwich variance, equals the lower-right
d_{\theta}\times d_{\theta}
block of limiting variance from display (
5.10
),

\begin{bmatrix}\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}&&\bm{0}\\
\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}&&\dot{\Psi}^{*}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\Sigma_{1:T}\begin{bmatrix}\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}&&\bm{0}\\
\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}&&\dot{\Psi}^{*}\end{bmatrix}^{-1,\top}.

(5.30)

By Proposition
1
(Blockwise Inversion of Matrices),

\begin{bmatrix}\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}&&\bm{0}\\
\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}&&\dot{\Psi}^{*}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}\big\{\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}\big\}^{-1}&&\bm{0}\\
-\big\{\dot{\Psi}^{*}\big\}^{-1}\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}\big\{\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}\big\}^{-1}&&\big\{\dot{\Psi}^{*}\big\}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}.

(5.31)

The matrices
M_{t}\in\real^{d_{\theta}\times d_{t}}
in
\Sigma^{\mathrm{adapt}}
above are defined as the lower left block of the inverse matrix above in display (
5.31
):

\big[M_{1},M_{2},\dots,M_{T-1}\big]\triangleq-\big\{\dot{\Psi}^{*}\big\}^{-1}\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}\big\{\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}\big\}^{-1}\in\real^{d_{\theta}\times d_{1:T-1}}.

(5.32)

Above we use
d_{1:T-1}\triangleq\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}d_{t}
. It is clear from the definition of
M_{t}
from display (
5.32
) above that if
\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}=\bm{0}
(i.e., the policy invariance property holds), then
\Sigma^{\mathrm{adapt}}=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\big[\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta^{*}\big)^{\otimes 2}\big]
, and the limiting sandwich and adaptive sandwich variances are equivalent.

Note that the asymptotic normality result with the standard sandwich variance for adaptively sampled longitudinal data does not follow from existing results (see Section
4
for further discussion of related work). Additionally, when the limiting standard and adaptive sandwich variances are equal, their variance estimators in general will not be equal in small samples (see the formulas for both the sandwich and adaptive sandwich variance estimators in Appendix
A.1.3
). In general, we advocate for using the adaptive sandwich variance over the standard sandwich variance since it is rare in digital intervention experiments for inference models to be exactly correctly specified.

6
Simulation Results

6.1
Data Generation

We consider a binary action setting with
T=50
decision times. We use a Boltzmann (or Softmax) exploration type adaptive sampling algorithm
Asadi and Littman 2017
;
Cesa-Bianchi et al. 2017
;
Sutton and Barto 2018
. The state used by the algorithm is the previous time step’s reward, i.e.,
S_{t}^{(i)}=\big[1,R^{(i)}_{t-1}\big]
. Specifically, the adaptive sampling algorithm forms action selection probabilities as follows:

\pi_{t}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t-1}\big)=\textnormal{Clip}_{0.1}\left[\textnormal{expit}\big(\rho\cdot\beta_{t-1,1}^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\big)\right],

(6.1)

where
\textnormal{Clip}_{0.1}[x]\triangleq\min(\max(x,0.1),0.9)
. We can interpret
\beta_{t-1,1}^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}
above as the adaptive sampling algorithm’s working model of a treatment effect. Above the parameter
\rho
is a positive constant that controls the steepness of the Softmax function; larger values of
\rho
make the Softmax function steeper. We vary the value of
\rho
in our experiments.
The policy parameter estimators
\hat{\beta}_{t}^{(n)}=\big[\hat{\beta}_{t,0}^{(n)},\hat{\beta}_{t,1}^{(n)}\big]
are those from the least squares example defined earlier in display (
3.8
).

We generate the rewards as follows:

R^{(i)}_{t}=\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{1}\bigg[\frac{1}{c_{\gamma}}\sum_{t^{\prime}=1}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-1-t^{\prime}}A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}\bigg]+\kappa_{2}A_{t}^{(i)}+\epsilon^{(i)}_{t}.

(6.2)

Above, the errors
\epsilon^{(i)}_{t}
are generated so that they are correlated over time within a user. We use
\gamma=0.95
, so
\sum_{t^{\prime}=1}^{t-1}\gamma^{t-1-t^{\prime}}A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}
is a discounted sum of the user’s recent “dosage”, i.e., the number of times action
A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}=1
was previously chosen for that user. We create this dosage variable because the impact of dosage on user receptivity is of great interest in mobile health trials
Liao et al. 2020
;
Trella et al. 2022
.
The dosage is normalized by
c_{\gamma}\triangleq 1/(1-\gamma)
to ensure the variable is between
[0,1]
. In our experiments, we vary the magnitude of the dosage coefficient
\kappa_{1}
. Note that the reward model used by the adaptive sampling algorithm, from display (
3.8
), is incorrectly specified, since it does not take dosage into account.
See Appendix
A.1
for more details.

6.2
Data Analysis

For inference we use the following choice of
\psi
, which corresponds to a least squares criterion:

\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta\big)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\big(R^{(i)}_{t}-\theta_{0}^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}-\theta_{1}A_{t}^{(i)}\big)\begin{bmatrix}S_{t}^{(i)}\\
A_{t}^{(i)}\end{bmatrix}.

(6.3)

Above
\theta_{1}\in\real
parameterizes the marginal treatment effect and
\theta_{0}\in\real^{2}
parameterize the model of the reward under
A_{t}^{(i)}=0
. Note that the data analysis model is misspecified because it does not take into account the dosage variable, which was used to generate rewards as described in display (
6.2
). Thus, the parameters
\theta_{1}^{*}
and
\theta_{0}^{*}
are projections. When we vary the coefficient for the dosage variable,
\kappa_{1}
, we can see how results are affected by the degree of model misspecification.

We construct
95\%
confidence intervals for
\theta_{1}^{*}
, the projection of the treatment effect. We compare the empirical coverage of confidence intervals constructed using both the standard sandwich and adaptive sandwich variance estimators. We include the formulas for both the sandwich and adaptive sandwich variance estimators in Appendix
A.1.3
.

6.3
Discussion of Results

As seen in Table
1
, the adaptive sandwich estimator consistently outperforms the standard sandwich variance estimator across all sample sizes and all simulation variants. Moreover, the performance gap increases with the magnitude of the dosage coefficient
\kappa_{1}
. This pattern is expected because as we discussed in Section
5.4
, the adaptive and sandwich variances are equivalent when the inference model
\psi
is correctly specified; increasing the magnitude of
\kappa_{1}
in the generative model increases the degree of model misspecification for our inference model from display (
6.3
) because it does not include dosage.

Additionally, note that the performance gap between the sandwich and adaptive sandwich variances increases with the algorithm’s Softmax steepness parameter
\rho
. Note that when
\rho=0
, then there is no adaptive sampling because the action selection probabilities from display (
6.1
) always equal
0.5
, i.e.,
\pi_{t}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t-1}\big)=0.5
a.s. As one increases the value of
\rho
, the steeper the Softmax curve becomes, and the more “adaptive” the algorithm is allowed to be (the algorithm is able to make greater changes in the action selection probabilities). Specifically, when we increase
\rho
, we expect the norm of the matrices
V_{T,t}
from display (
5.28
) to grow. Recall that

V_{T,t}\triangleq\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\beta_{t}}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\left[W_{t+1}^{(i)}(\beta_{t},\beta_{t}^{*})\psi(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta^{*})\right]\bigg|_{\beta_{t}=\beta_{t}^{*}}\in\real^{d_{\theta}\times d_{t}}

captures how the expectation of the estimating function changes with small changes in the policy parameter
\beta_{t}
. Note that as discussed in Section
5.4
, when
V_{T,t}=0
for all
t\in[1\colon T-1]
, then the limiting sandwich and adaptive sandwich variances are equivalent.

7
Discussion

On adaptively sampled data the error of
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}
in estimating the target policy
\pi_{t}^{*}
impacts what data is collected at the
t^{\textnormal{th}}
decision time, and thus the error of future estimated policies (
\hat{\pi}_{t^{\prime}}^{(n)}
for
t^{\prime}>t
) and the final Z-estimator
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
. A key conceptual contribution of this work is to provide an approach to represent how the errors in the estimated policies impact the final Z-estimator
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
. In particular, we show that
\hat{\beta}_{t-1}^{(n)}
, which parameterizes the estimated policy
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}
, can be treated like a plug-in estimator for
\beta_{t-1}^{*}
that was fit on the same dataset used to form
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
. In other words, even though on adaptively sampled data the estimated policy parameters affect the
data collection
, they can be handled analogously to plug-in estimators for nuisance parameters that are used only in the
data analysis
.

The greatest limitation of this work is that it does not apply to adaptively sampled batch datasets in which the policies used to collect the data are (i) not smooth in their parameters or (ii) allow the amount of exploration to go to zero. As discussed in Section
5.2.1
, many common RL algorithms for bandit and Markov decision process settings do not satisfy our smoothness and exploration conditions. However, the studies in digital interventions motivating this work will use adaptive sampling algorithms that do satisfy these conditions.

Future work includes deriving efficient estimators based on adaptively sampled data and designing algorithms that are able to effectively pool over heterogeneous users. Another direction for future work will be to allow the estimating function itself,
\psi
to include the adaptive sampling action selection probabilities. For example, estimating functions used in off-policy analyses commonly include the action selection probabilities used to collect the data
Jiang and Li 2016
;
Kallus and Uehara 2020
;
Thomas and Brunskill 2016
. More generally, it would be of interest to extend this work to allow the estimating function
\psi
to include different types of plug-in estimators, e.g., plug-in estimates of the Q-function which are also often used in off-policy analyses
Jiang and Li 2016
;
Kallus and Uehara 2020
;
Thomas and Brunskill 2016
. There are also open questions about how to incorporate estimates formed by high-dimensional machine learning models to potentially increase the efficiency of estimators in these settings.

Overview of Appendices

Appendix
A
: Examples and Simulation Details.

This appendix includes formulas for variance estimators, example RL algorithms that satisfy Conditions
2
and
3
, and Lemmas pertaining to bracketing numbers.

•

Appendix
B
: Policy Parameter Results.
This appendix includes consistency and stochastic equicontinuity results for policy parameters
\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}
.

•

Appendix
C
: Main Asymptotic Results.

This appendix includes proofs of consistency of
\hat{\theta}^{(n)}
(Theorem
5.1
) and asymptotic normality of
\sqrt{n}\big(\hat{\theta}^{(n)}-\theta^{*}\big)
(Theorem
5.2
).

•

Appendix
D
: Limit Theorems for Adaptively Collected Data.

This appendix builds up results to show functional weak Law of Large Number and functional asymptotic normality results for Radon-Nikodym weighted empirical processes on adaptively collected data.

•

Appendix
E
: Maximal Inequalities for Adaptively Collected Data.

This appendix builds up results to show a maximal inequality for Radon-Nikodym weighted empirical processes on adaptively collected data for function classes with finite bracketing integrals.

A.1
Simulation Details

A.1.1
Additional Information on Reward Generation

In the reward generation formula from display (
6.2
), for each user
i\in[1\colon n]
, the errors
\epsilon_{t}^{(i)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
marginally for each
t\in[1\colon T]
; however,
\textnormal{Corr}(\epsilon_{t}^{(i)},\epsilon_{s}^{(i)})=0.5^{|t-s|/2}
, which means the reward errors within a user are correlated over time.
Additionally, we set the parameters from display (
6.2
) to the following values:
\kappa_{0}=0
,
\kappa_{2}=0
, and we consider simulations with both
\kappa_{1}=1
and
\kappa_{1}=5
.

A.1.2
Sandwich Variance Estimator

Recall from display (
5.1
) that the sandwich variance is
[\dot{\Psi}^{*}]^{-1}\Sigma[\dot{\Psi}^{*}]^{-1,\top}
, where

\dot{\Psi}^{*}\triangleq\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\theta}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\left[\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta\big)\right]\bigg|_{\theta=\theta^{*}}\textnormal{~~~~~~and~~~~~~}\Sigma\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\left[\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta^{*}\big)^{\otimes 2}\right].

(A.1)

Above, we use the notation
x^{\otimes 2}\triangleq xx^{\top}
.

The sandwich variance
estimator
we use is
\underline{\dot{\Psi}}^{-1,\top}\hat{\Sigma}\underline{\dot{\Psi}}^{-1,\top}
, where

\underline{\dot{\Psi}}\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\theta}}\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta\big)\bigg|_{\theta=\hat{\theta}^{(n)}}\textnormal{~~~~~~and~~~~~~}\hat{\Sigma}\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\hat{\theta}^{(n)}\big)^{\otimes 2}.

(A.2)

A.1.3
Adaptive Sandwich Variance Estimator

Recall from display (
5.2
) that the sandwich variance is
[\dot{\Psi}^{*}]^{-1}\Sigma^{\mathrm{adapt}}[\dot{\Psi}^{*}]^{-1,\top}
, where
\dot{\Psi}^{*}
is defined as in display (
A.1
) and

\Sigma^{\mathrm{adapt}}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\bigg[\bigg\{\psi\big(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta^{*}\big)+\dot{\Psi}^{*}\sum_{t=1}^{T-1}M_{t}~\phi_{t}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t}^{*}\big)\bigg\}^{\otimes 2}\bigg].

By Lemma
C.1
, the adaptive sandwich variance
[\dot{\Psi}^{*}]^{-1}\Sigma^{\mathrm{adapt}}[\dot{\Psi}^{*}]^{-1,\top}
equals the lower-right
\textnormal{dim}(\theta)\times\textnormal{dim}(\theta)
block of the following matrix:

\begin{bmatrix}\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}&&\bm{0}\\
\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}&&\dot{\Psi}^{*}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\Sigma_{1:T}\begin{bmatrix}\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}&&\bm{0}\\
\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}&&\dot{\Psi}^{*}\end{bmatrix}^{-1,\top}

(A.3)

where

\Sigma_{1:T}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\left[\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{(i)};\beta_{1}^{*})\\
\phi_{2}(\mathcal{H}_{2}^{(i)};\beta_{2}^{*})\\
\vdots\\
\phi_{T-1}(\mathcal{H}_{T-1}^{(i)};\beta_{T-1}^{*})\\
\psi(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\theta^{*})\end{pmatrix}^{\otimes 2}\right].

and

\begin{bmatrix}\dot{\Phi}_{1:T-1}^{*}&&\bm{0}\\
\bm{V}_{T,1:T-1}&&\dot{\Psi}^{*}\end{bmatrix}\triangleq\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\beta_{1:T-1}}}\Phi_{1:T-1}(\beta_{1:T-1})&&\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\theta}}\Phi_{1:T-1}(\beta_{1:T-1})\\
\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\beta_{1:T-1}}}\Psi{}(\beta_{1:T-1},\theta)&&\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\theta}}\Psi{}(\beta_{1:T-1},\theta)\end{bmatrix}\bigg|_{(\beta_{1:T-1},\theta)=(\beta_{1:T-1}^{*},\theta^{*})}.

Note above that
\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\theta}}\Phi_{1:T-1}(\beta_{1:T-1})=0
since
\Phi_{1:T-1}(\beta_{1:T-1})
is not a function of
\theta
. We estimate the entire matrix from display (
A.3
) as follows:

\begin{bmatrix}\underline{\dot{\Phi}}_{1:T-1}&&\bm{0}\\
\hat{\bm{V}}_{T,1:T-1}&&\underline{\dot{\Psi}}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\hat{\Sigma}_{1:T}\begin{bmatrix}\underline{\dot{\Phi}}_{1:T-1}&&\bm{0}\\
\hat{\bm{V}}_{T,1:T-1}&&\underline{\dot{\Psi}}\end{bmatrix}^{-1,\top}

where
\underline{\dot{\Psi}}
is defined in display (
A.2
),

\underline{\dot{\Phi}}_{1:T-1}\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\beta_{1:T-1}}}\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{(i)};\beta_{1})\\
W_{2}^{(i)}(\beta_{1},\hat{\beta}_{1}^{(n)})\phi_{2}(\mathcal{H}_{2}^{(i)};\beta_{2})\\
W_{2:3}^{(i)}(\beta_{1:2},\hat{\beta}_{1:2}^{(n)})\phi_{3}(\mathcal{H}_{3}^{(i)};\beta_{3})\\
\vdots\\
W_{2:T-1}^{(i)}(\beta_{1:T-2},\hat{\beta}_{1:T-2}^{(n)})\phi_{T-1}(\mathcal{H}_{T-1}^{(i)};\beta_{T-1})\end{bmatrix}\bigg|_{\beta_{1:T-1}=\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}},

\hat{\bm{V}}_{T,1:T-1}\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigg\{\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\beta_{1:T-1}}}W_{2:T}^{(i)}(\beta_{1:T-1},\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)})\bigg\}\bigg|_{\beta_{1:T-1}=\hat{\beta}_{1:T-1}^{(n)}}\psi(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\hat{\theta}^{(n)}),

and

\hat{\Sigma}_{1:T}\triangleq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{1}^{(i)};\hat{\beta}_{1}^{(n)})\\
\phi_{2}(\mathcal{H}_{2}^{(i)};\hat{\beta}_{2}^{(n)})\\
\vdots\\
\phi_{T-1}(\mathcal{H}_{T-1}^{(i)};\hat{\beta}_{T-1}^{(n)})\\
\psi(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)};\hat{\theta}^{(n)})\end{pmatrix}^{\otimes 2}.

A.2
Example Algorithms that Satisfy Conditions
2
and
3

A.2.1
Boltzmann Exploration Algorithm (Lemma
A.1
)

We consider the Boltzmann (or Softmax) exploration type adaptive sampling algorithm
Asadi and Littman 2017
,
Cesa-Bianchi et al. 2017
,
Sutton and Barto 2018
as described in Section
6
(Simulation Results). Specifically, we consider a binary action setting (
\mathcal{A}=\{0,1\}
) and a Boltzmann sampling algorithm that forms action selection probabilities as follows:

\pi_{t}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t-1}\big)=\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}\left[\textnormal{expit}\big(\rho\cdot\beta_{t-1,1}^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\big)\right],

(A.4)

where
\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}[x]\triangleq\min(\max(x,\pi_{\min}),1-\pi_{\min})
. Above the parameter
\rho
is a positive constant that controls the steepness of the Softmax function; larger values of
\rho
make the Softmax function steeper.
The policy parameters
\hat{\beta}_{t}^{(n)}=\big[\hat{\beta}_{t,0}^{(n)},\hat{\beta}_{t,1}^{(n)}\big]
are those from the least squares example defined earlier in display (
3.8
).

Note that exploration Condition
2
is satisfied because the action selection probabilities are constrained between
[\pi_{\min},1-\pi_{\min}]
. In Lemma
A.1
below, we show that Condition
3
holds under the assumption that
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\big[\big\|S_{t}^{(i)}\big\|_{2}^{2+\alpha}\big]<\infty
for all
t\in[2\colon T]
.

Lemma A.1

(Boltzmann Exploration Algorithm)
.

We consider the Boltzmann algorithm example that selects actions as described in display (
A.4
). We show that Condition
3
holds under the condition that
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\big[\big\|S_{t}^{(i)}\big\|_{2}^{2+\alpha}\big]<\infty
for all
t\in[2\colon T]
.

Proof of Lemma
A.1
.

Note that for any
\beta_{1:t-1}\in\real^{d_{1:t-1}}
,

\big|\pi_{t}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{1:t-1}\big)-\pi_{t}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{1:t-1}^{*}\big)\big|

=\bigg|\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}\left[\textnormal{expit}\big(\rho\cdot\beta_{t-1,1}^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\big)\right]-\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}\left[\textnormal{expit}\big(\rho\cdot(\beta_{t-1,1}^{*})^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\big)\right]\bigg|.

(A.5)

Note that for any real numbers
x,y
that
\big|\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}(x)-\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}(y)\big|\leq|x-y|
. This is because

•

If
x,y\in[\pi_{\min},1-\pi_{\min}]
, then
\big|\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}(x)-\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}(y)\big|=|x-y|
.

•

If
x,y<\pi_{\min}
or
x,y>1-\pi_{\min}
, then
0=\big|\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}(x)-\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}(y)\big|\leq|x-y|
.

•

If
x>\pi_{\min}
and
y<\pi_{\min}
, then
\big|\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}(x)-\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}(y)\big|\leq x-\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}(y)<x-y=|x-y|
. Same argument goes for the case that
y>\pi_{\min}
and
x<\pi_{\min}
.

•

If
x<1-\pi_{\min}
and
y>1-\pi_{\min}
, then
\big|\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}(x)-\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}(y)\big|\leq\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}(y)-x<y-x=|x-y|
. Same argument goes for the case that
y<1-\pi_{\min}
and
x>1-\pi_{\min}
.

Thus,

\leq\left|\textnormal{expit}\big(\rho\cdot\beta_{t-1,1}^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\big)-\textnormal{expit}\big(\rho\cdot(\beta_{t-1,1}^{*})^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\big)\right|

(A.6)

Note that for any function
f:\mathcal{X}\mapsto[0,1]
, for any
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
,

|f(x)-f(x^{\prime})|\leq\sup_{x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}}\bigg|\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x}}f(x)\big|_{x=x_{0}}\bigg||x-x^{\prime}|.

Above
\sup_{x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}}\big|\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x}}f(x)\big|_{x=x_{0}}\big|
is the maximum absolute value of the derivative of
f
.
We can use the above observation to upper bound display (
A.6
) as follows:

\leq\sup_{x_{0}\in\real}\bigg|\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x}}\textnormal{expit}(x)\big|_{x=x_{0}}\bigg|\cdot\big|\rho\cdot\beta_{t-1,1}^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}-\rho\cdot(\beta_{t-1,1}^{*})^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\big|

(A.7)

Note that
\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x}}\textnormal{expit}(x)=\textnormal{expit}(x)\big\{1-\textnormal{expit}(x)\big\}
and that
\sup_{p\in[0,1]}p(1-p)=0.25
. Thus,

\sup_{x_{0}\in\real}\bigg|\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x}}\textnormal{expit}(x)\big|_{x=x_{0}}\bigg|=\sup_{x_{0}\in\real}\left|\textnormal{expit}(x_{0})\big\{1-\textnormal{expit}(x_{0})\big\}\right|\leq\sup_{p\in[0,1]}\big|p(1-p)\big|\leq 0.25.

Thus, we can upper bound display (
A.7
) with the following

\leq 0.25\big|\rho\cdot\beta_{t-1,1}^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}-\rho\cdot(\beta_{t-1,1}^{*})^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\big|

=0.25\rho\big|\beta_{t-1,1}^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}-(\beta_{t-1,1}^{*})^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\big|

By Cauchy Schwartz inequality,

\leq 0.25\rho\big\|\beta_{t-1,1}-\beta_{t-1,1}^{*}\big\|_{2}\big\|S_{t}^{(i)}\big\|_{2}.

By the above result, Condition
3
holds since
\mathbb{E}\left[\big\|S_{t}^{(i)}\big\|_{2}^{2+\alpha}\right]<\infty
.
\blacksquare

A.2.2
Stochastic Mirror Descent Algorithm (Lemma
A.2
)

We now give an example of an online stochastic mirror descent algorithm, based on those from

[
Lattimore and Szepesvári 2020
, pg 361]
and

Bubeck et al. 2012
, whose policy class satisfies Conditions
2
and
3
. We assume a binary action setting with
\mathcal{A}=\{0,1\}
and assume that
\hat{\beta}_{t-1}^{(n)}=[\hat{\beta}_{t-1,0}^{(n)},\hat{\beta}_{t-1,1}^{(n)}]
below are estimated using the least squares criterion from display (
3.8
).

Note that for online stochastic mirror descent algorithms,
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}
is an updated version of
\hat{\pi}_{t-1}^{(n)}
, which itself is an updated version of
\hat{\pi}_{t-2}^{(n)}
, and so on. This means that parameters of the class
\pi_{t}
must include those of
\pi_{t-1},\pi_{t-2},\dots,\pi_{2}
. We will use slightly non-standard notation to represent this,
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}(\,\cdot\,)=\pi_{t}(\,\cdot\,;\hat{\beta}_{1:t-1}^{(n)})
, where each
\hat{\beta}_{t-1}^{(n)}=[\hat{\beta}_{t-1,0}^{(n)},\hat{\beta}_{t-1,1}^{(n)}]
is estimated using the least squares criterion from display (
3.8
). Since we consider a binary action setting, to characterize a policy it is sufficient to define the probability that action
1
is selected in each state.

\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)}\big)=\pi_{t}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)};\hat{\beta}_{1:t-1}^{(n)}\big)\\
=\textnormal{argmin}_{p\in[\pi_{\min},1-\pi_{\min}]}\left\{\eta_{t}\big(-\hat{\beta}_{t-1,0}^{(n),\top}S_{t}^{(i)}-p~\hat{\beta}_{t-1,1}^{(n),\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\big)+\big(\hat{\pi}_{t-1}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)}\big)-p\big)^{2}\right\}.

(A.8)

Above,
\eta_{t}>0
is a learning rate and
\pi_{\min}\in(0,0.5]
is the minimum exploration rate. Note that
\hat{\beta}_{t-1,0}^{(n),\top}S_{t}^{(i)}+p~\hat{\beta}_{t-1,1}^{(n),\top}S_{t}^{(i)}
is an estimate of the expectation of
R^{(i)}_{t}
given
S_{t}^{(i)},\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(i)}
when
A_{t}^{(i)}
is selected with probability
p
. Since the algorithm is designed to minimize a loss, we multiply
\hat{\beta}_{t-1,0}^{(n),\top}S_{t}^{(i)}+p~\hat{\beta}_{t-1,1}^{(n),\top}S_{t}^{(i)}
by minus
1
to ensure the algorithm is maximizing the reward (i.e., minimizing the negative reward). The term
\big(\hat{\pi}_{t-1}(1,S_{t}^{(i)})-p\big)^{2}
is a Bregman divergence and can be replaced by other Bregman divergences, e.g., KL-divergence.

By display (
A.8
) above, we can derive

\pi_{t}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)};\hat{\beta}_{1:t-1}^{(n)}\big)=\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}\left(\pi_{t-1}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)};\hat{\beta}_{1:t-2}^{(n)}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{t}\hat{\beta}_{t-1,1}^{(n),\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\right),

(A.9)

where
\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}(x)\triangleq\min\big(\max\big(x,\pi_{\min}\big),1-\pi_{\min}\big)
; see Lemma
A.2
below for proof.

Note that exploration Condition
2
is satisfied because the action selection probabilities are constrained between
[\pi_{\min},1-\pi_{\min}]
.
We can also show that Condition
3
holds because

\big|\pi_{t}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{1:t-1}\big)-\pi_{t}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{1:t-1}^{*}\big)\big|\leq\frac{1}{2}\eta_{t}\big\|S_{t}^{(i)}\big\|~\big\|\beta_{t-1,1}-\beta_{t-1,1}^{*}\big\|

(A.10)

for any
\beta_{1:t-1}\in\real^{d_{1:t-1}}
, where
d_{1:t-1}\triangleq\sum_{t^{\prime}=1}^{t-1}d_{t^{\prime}}
; we also show this in Lemma
A.2
below.

Lemma A.2

(Stochastic Mirror Descent Algorithm)
.

We consider the stochastic mirror descent algorithm example that selects actions as described in display (
A.8
). We show that display (
A.9
) holds. We also show that Condition
3
holds under the conditions that

(a)
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\big[\big\|S_{t}^{(i)}\big\|_{2}^{2+\alpha}\big]<\infty
for all
t\in[2\colon T]
(the constant
\alpha>0
is the same as that from Condition
3
), and

(b) the learning rates are bounded, i.e., for a constant
\eta_{\max}
,
\eta_{t}\leq\eta_{\max}<\infty
for all
t\in[1\colon T]
.

Proof of Lemma
A.2
.

Showing display (
A.9
) holds.

Recall from display (
A.8
) that the stochastic mirror descent algorithm uses the following action selection probabilities:

\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)}\big)=\pi_{t}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)};\hat{\beta}_{1:t-1}^{(n)}\big)\\
=\textnormal{argmin}_{p\in[\pi_{\min},1-\pi_{\min}]}\left\{\eta_{t}\big(-\hat{\beta}_{t-1,0}^{(n),\top}S_{t}^{(i)}-p~\hat{\beta}_{t-1,1}^{(n),\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\big)+\big(\hat{\pi}_{t-1}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)}\big)-p\big)^{2}\right\}.

By taking the derivative of the following criterion with respect to
p
,

-\eta_{t}\big(\hat{\beta}_{t-1,0}^{(n),\top}S_{t}^{(i)}+p~\hat{\beta}_{t-1,1}^{(n),\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\big)+\big(\hat{\pi}_{t-1}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)}\big)-p\big)^{2},

(A.11)

we have

-\eta_{t}~\hat{\beta}_{t-1,1}^{(n),\top}S_{t}^{(i)}-2\big\{\hat{\pi}_{t-1}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)}\big)-p\big\}.

Since the second derivative of the criterion from display (
A.11
) with respect to
p
is
2>0
, the global minimizer of the criterion (not restricted to
[\pi_{\min},1-\pi_{\min}]
) is
p=\hat{\pi}_{t-1}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{t}~\hat{\beta}_{t-1,1}^{(n),\top}S_{t}^{(i)}
. Also note that the criterion from Equation (
A.11
) is convex because its derivative is strictly increasing in
p
. Note that the constrained minimizer of a convex function either equals the global minimizer or is on the boundary of the constraint space. Thus we have that the constrained minimizer,
\hat{\pi}_{t}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)}\big)
, equals the following:

\pi_{t}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)};\hat{\beta}_{1:t-1}^{(n)}\big)=\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}\bigg(\hat{\pi}_{t-1}(1,S_{t}^{(i)})+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{t}\hat{\beta}_{t-1,1}^{(n),\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\bigg),

where
\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}(x)\triangleq\min\big(\max(x,\pi_{\min}),1-\pi_{\min}\big)
. Thus, we have shown that display (
A.9
) holds.

Showing Condition
3
holds.

Note that for any
\beta_{1:t-1}\in\real^{d_{1:t-1}}
,

\big|\pi_{t}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{1:t-1}\big)-\pi_{t}\big(1,S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{1:t-1}^{*}\big)\big|

=\bigg|\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}\bigg(\hat{\pi}_{t-1}(1,S_{t}^{(i)})+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{t}\beta_{t-1,1}^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\bigg)-\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}\bigg(\hat{\pi}_{t-1}(1,S_{t}^{(i)})+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{t}\beta_{t-1,1}^{*,\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\bigg)\bigg|.

Note that for any real numbers
x,y
that
\big|\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}(x)-\textnormal{Clip}_{\pi_{\min}}(y)\big|\leq|x-y|
; the justification for this is discussed below display (
A.5
).
Thus, we have that display (
A.5
) can be upper bounded by the following:

\leq\left|\hat{\pi}_{t-1}(1,S_{t}^{(i)})+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{t}\beta_{t-1,1}^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}-\hat{\pi}_{t-1}(1,S_{t}^{(i)})-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{t}\beta_{t-1,1}^{*,\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\right|

=\left|\frac{1}{2}\eta_{t}\left(\beta_{t-1,1}-\beta_{t-1,1}^{*}\right)^{\top}S_{t}^{(i)}\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}\eta_{t}\big\|S_{t}^{(i)}\big\|_{2}~\big\|\beta_{t-1,1}-\beta_{t-1,1}^{*}\big\|_{2}

The last inequality above holds by Cauchy-Schwartz.
By the above, Condition
3
holds since
\mathbb{E}\left[\big\|S_{t}^{(i)}\big\|_{2}^{2+\alpha}\right]<\infty
and
\eta_{t}\leq\eta_{\max}<\infty
for all
t\in[1\colon T]
.
\blacksquare

A.3
Radon-Nikodym Derivatives (Lemma
A.3
)

Lemma A.3

(Radon-Nikodym Derivatives)
.

For any
\beta_{t-1}\in\real^{d_{t-1}}
, conditional on any
S_{t}^{(i)}
,
\mu(\,\cdot\,)\triangleq\pi_{t}\big(\,\cdot\,,S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t-1}\big)
defines a probability measure on the sigma-algebra
\sigma(A_{t}^{(i)})
. Also conditional on any
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(1:n)}
and
S_{t}^{(i)}
, the policy
\nu(\,\cdot\,)\triangleq\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}\big(\,\cdot\,,S_{t}^{(i)}\big)
defines a probability measure on the sigma-algebra
\sigma(A_{t}^{(i)})
.

Under Condition
2
, conditionally on almost every
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(1:n)}
and
S_{t}^{(i)}
,
g(\,\cdot\,)\triangleq\frac{\pi_{t}(\,\cdot\,,S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t-1})}{\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}(\,\cdot\,,S_{t}^{(i)})}
is a Radon-Nikodym derivative, i.e., for any measurable subset
\bar{A}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, conditionally on almost every
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(1:n)}
and
S_{t}^{(i)}
,
\mu(\bar{A})=\int_{\bar{A}}g~d\nu
.

Proof of Lemma
A.3
.

We first show that that conditionally on almost every
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(1:n)}
and
S_{t}^{(i)}
,
\mu(\,\cdot\,)=\pi_{t}\big(\,\cdot\,,S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t-1}\big)
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\nu(\,\cdot\,)=\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}\big(\,\cdot\,,S_{t}^{(i)}\big)
.

By exploration Condition
2
we have that for any measurable subset measurable subset
\bar{A}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
,
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}\big(\bar{A},S_{t}^{(i)}\big)\geq\pi_{\min}>0
a.s. This means that for any measurable subset
\bar{A}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
,
\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}\big(\bar{A},S_{t}^{(i)}\big)\geq\pi_{\min}>0
conditionally on almost every
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(1:n)}
and
S_{t}^{(i)}
. Thus we have that conditional on almost every
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(1:n)}
and
S_{t}^{(i)}
,
\mu(\,\cdot\,)=\pi_{t}\big(\,\cdot\,,S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t-1}\big)
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\nu(\,\cdot\,)=\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}\big(\,\cdot\,,S_{t}^{(i)}\big)
.

Thus, for some function
g:\mathcal{A}\mapsto[0,\infty)
, conditionally on almost every
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(1:n)}
and
S_{t}^{(i)}
,
\mu(\bar{A})=\int_{\bar{A}}g~d\nu
for any measurable subset
\bar{A}\subseteq\sigma(\mathcal{A})
. This means that conditionally on almost every
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}^{(1:n)}
and
S_{t}^{(i)}
,

\pi_{t}\big(\bar{A},S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t-1}\big)=\int_{\bar{A}}g~d\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}(\,\cdot\,,S_{t}^{(i)}).

For
g(\,\cdot\,)=\frac{\pi_{t}(\,\cdot\,,S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t-1})}{\hat{\pi}_{t}^{(n)}(\,\cdot\,,S_{t}^{(i)})}
, the above equality is satisfied.
\blacksquare

A.4
Bracketing Numbers

A.4.1
Definition of Bracketing Numbers

Following the notation used in Chapter 19 of
Van der Vaart 2000
, for any function class
\mathcal{F}
of real-valued functions of
\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)}
, we use
N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)
to denote the number of brackets of size
\epsilon
in
L_{P}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})
norm needed to cover
\mathcal{F}
. Formally, this means we can find
N_{\epsilon}\triangleq N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)
number of brackets or pairs of real-valued functions of
\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)}
,
\big\{(l_{k},u_{k})\big\}_{k=1}^{N_{\epsilon}}
, such that (i) all brackets together cover
\mathcal{F}
, i.e., for any
f\in\mathcal{F}
we can find some bracket
(l_{k},u_{k})
such that
l_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})\leq f(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})\leq u_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})
a.s., and (ii) the brackets have size less than
\epsilon
, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\big[\big|u_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})-l_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})\big|^{p}\big]^{1/p}<\epsilon
. As done in Chapter 19 of
Van der Vaart 2000
, we assume that the bracketing functions themselves also have finite
L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})
norm, i.e., for any
\epsilon>0
and
k\in[1\colon N_{\epsilon}]
,
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\big[|u_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})|^{p}\big]^{1/p}<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:T}^{*}}\big[|l_{k}(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{(i)})|^{p}\big]^{1/p}<\infty
.

A.4.2
Product of Lipschitz Policy Functions are Lipschitz (Lemma
A.4
)

Lemma A.4

(Product of Lipschitz Policy Functions are Lipschitz)
.

Let
t\in[3\colon T-1]
. Under Condition
3
(Lipschitz Policy Function), for any
\beta_{1:t-1},\tilde{\beta}_{1:t-1}\in B_{1:t-1}
,

\bigg|\prod_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\pi_{t^{\prime}}\big(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)};\beta_{t^{\prime}-1}\big)-\prod_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\pi_{t^{\prime}}\big(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)};\tilde{\beta}_{t^{\prime}-1}\big)\bigg|\\
\leq\bigg\{\sum_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\dot{\pi}_{t^{\prime}}\big(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}\big)\bigg\}\big\|\beta_{1:t-1}-\tilde{\beta}_{1:t-1}\big\|_{2}\textnormal{~~~a.s.}

(A.12)

Proof of Lemma
A.4
.

Note that by telescoping series,

\prod_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\pi_{t^{\prime}}\big(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)};\beta_{t^{\prime}-1}\big)-\prod_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\pi_{t^{\prime}}\big(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)};\tilde{\beta}_{t^{\prime}-1}\big)

=\left[\pi_{2}(A_{2}^{(i)},S_{2}^{(i)};\beta_{1})-\pi_{2}(A_{2}^{(i)},S_{2}^{(i)};\tilde{\beta}_{1})\right]\bigg\{\prod_{t^{\prime}=3}^{t}\pi_{t^{\prime}}(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)};\beta_{t^{\prime}-1})\bigg\}\\
+\left\{\pi_{2}(A_{2}^{(i)},S_{2}^{(i)};\tilde{\beta}_{1})\right\}\left[\pi_{3}(A_{3}^{(i)},S_{3}^{(i)};\beta_{2})-\pi_{3}(A_{3}^{(i)},S_{3}^{(i)};\tilde{\beta}_{2})\right]\bigg\{\prod_{t^{\prime}=4}^{t}\pi_{t^{\prime}}(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)};\beta_{t^{\prime}-1})\bigg\}\\
+\ldots\\
+\bigg\{\prod_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t-1}\pi_{t^{\prime}}(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)};\tilde{\beta}_{t^{\prime}-1})\bigg\}\left[\pi_{t}(A_{t}^{(i)},S_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{t-1})-\pi_{t}(A_{t}^{(i)},S_{t}^{(i)};\tilde{\beta}_{t-1})\right]

=\sum_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\bigg\{\prod_{k=2}^{t^{\prime}-1}\pi_{k}(A_{k}^{(i)},S_{k}^{(i)};\tilde{\beta}_{k-1})\bigg\}\left[\pi_{t^{\prime}}(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)};\beta_{t^{\prime}-1})-\pi_{t^{\prime}}(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)};\tilde{\beta}_{t^{\prime}-1})\right]\\
\bigg\{\prod_{k=t^{\prime}+1}^{t}\pi_{k}(A_{k}^{(i)},S_{k}^{(i)};\beta_{k-1})\bigg\}

By slight abuse of notation, above we use
\prod_{k=2}^{1}\pi_{k}(A_{k}^{(i)},S_{k}^{(i)};\tilde{\beta}_{k-1})=1
and

\prod_{k=t+1}^{t}\pi_{k}(A_{k}^{(i)},S_{k}^{(i)};\beta_{k-1})=1
.

Using the above result and triangle inequality,

\bigg|\prod_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\pi_{t^{\prime}}\big(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)};\beta_{t^{\prime}-1}\big)-\prod_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\pi_{t^{\prime}}\big(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)};\tilde{\beta}_{t^{\prime}-1}\big)\bigg|

\leq\sum_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\bigg|\prod_{k=2}^{t^{\prime}-1}\pi_{k}(A_{k}^{(i)},S_{k}^{(i)};\tilde{\beta}_{k-1})\bigg|\left|\pi_{t^{\prime}}(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)};\beta_{t^{\prime}-1})-\pi_{t^{\prime}}(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)};\tilde{\beta}_{t^{\prime}-1})\right|\\
\bigg|\prod_{k=t^{\prime}+1}^{t}\pi_{k}(A_{k}^{(i)},S_{k}^{(i)};\beta_{k-1})\bigg|

Since the terms
\big|\pi_{k}(A_{k}^{(i)},S_{k}^{(i)};\beta_{k-1})\big|
and
\big|\pi_{k}(A_{k}^{(i)},S_{k}^{(i)};\tilde{\beta}_{k-1})\big|
are less than or equal to
1
a.s.,

\leq\sum_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\left|\pi_{t^{\prime}}(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)};\beta_{t^{\prime}-1})-\pi_{t^{\prime}}(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)};\tilde{\beta}_{t^{\prime}-1})\right|

By Condition
3
(Lipschitz Policy Function),

\leq\sum_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\dot{\pi}_{t^{\prime}}(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)})\big\|\beta_{t^{\prime}-1}-\tilde{\beta}_{t^{\prime}-1}\big\|_{2}

\leq\bigg\{\sum_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\dot{\pi}_{t^{\prime}}(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)})\bigg\}\big\|\beta_{1:t-1}-\tilde{\beta}_{1:t-1}\big\|_{2}

By the above argument, we have that

\bigg|\prod_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\pi_{t}(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)};\beta_{t^{\prime}-1})-\prod_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\pi_{t}(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)};\tilde{\beta}_{t^{\prime}-1})\bigg|\\
\leq\bigg\{\sum_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\dot{\pi}_{t^{\prime}}(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)})\bigg\}\big\|\beta_{1:t-1}-\tilde{\beta}_{1:t-1}\big\|_{2}\textnormal{~~~a.s.}~\blacksquare

(A.13)

A.4.3
Bracketing Number for Product of Function Classes (Lemma
A.5
)

Lemma A.5

(Bracketing Number for Product of Function Classes)
.

Let
t\in[2\colon T-1]
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a class of real-valued functions of
\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)}
indexed by
\lambda\in L\subseteq\real^{d_{L}}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{F}\triangleq\big\{f(\,\cdot\,;\lambda):\lambda\in L\big\}
. Also let
\Pi_{2:t}\triangleq\big\{\prod_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\pi_{t^{\prime}}\big(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t^{\prime}-1}\big)\textnormal{~~s.t.~~}\beta_{1:t-1}\in B_{1:t-1}\big\}
. Finally, also let

\Pi_{2:t}\cdot\mathcal{F}\triangleq\bigg\{\bigg[\prod_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\pi_{t^{\prime}}\big(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t^{\prime}-1}\big)\bigg]f(\,\cdot\,;\lambda)\textnormal{~~s.t.~~}\beta_{1:t-1}\in B_{1:t-1},\lambda\in L\bigg\}.

For any
p\geq 1
,
N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon,\Pi_{2:t}\cdot\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)<\infty
for all
\epsilon>0
under the following conditions:

(i)

Condition
3
(Lipschitz Policy Function) holds; if
p>2
, then Condition
3
must hold for
\alpha=p-2
.

(ii)

N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)<\infty
for all
\epsilon>0
.

(iii)

There exists a real-valued, measurable function
F
of
\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)}
such that (a)
\big|f(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\big|\leq F(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})
a.s. for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
, and (b)
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\big[\big|F(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\dot{\pi}_{t^{\prime}}(\mathcal{H}_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)})\big|^{p}\big]<\infty
for all
t^{\prime}\in[2\colon t]
, where the functions
\dot{\pi}_{t^{\prime}}
are from Condition
3
.

Furthermore,
\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon,\Pi_{2:t}\cdot\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)}d\epsilon<\infty
under the additional condition that

(iv)

\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)}d\epsilon<\infty

Note that assumption
(iv)
above implies that assumption
(ii)
holds.

Remark 8

(Bracketing Number for the Product of Policy and Estimating Functions)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{\Pi c^{\top}\psi}\big(B_{1:T-1},K_{\theta})\triangleq\left\{\big[\prod_{t=2}^{T}\pi_{t}(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t-1})\big]c^{\top}\psi(\,\cdot\,;\theta\big)\textnormal{~s.t.~}\beta_{1:T-1}\in B_{1:T-1},\theta\in K_{\theta}\right\}
for any compact set
K_{\theta}\subseteq\real^{d_{\theta}}
and any
c\in\real^{d_{\theta}}
. By Lemma
A.5
, we have the following results:

(a1)

Under Condition
3
and assumption
(C3)
,
\mathcal{F}_{\Pi c^{\top}\psi}\big(B_{1:T-1},K_{\theta})
has a finite bracketing number, i.e., that
N_{[~]}\left(\epsilon,~\mathcal{F}_{\Pi c^{\top}\psi}(B_{1:T-1},K_{\theta}),~L_{1+\alpha}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\right)<\infty
for any
\epsilon>0
.

(a2)

Under Condition
3
and assumption
(N4)
,
\mathcal{F}_{\Pi c^{\top}\psi}\big(B_{1:T-1},\Theta)
has a finite bracketing integral, i.e., that
\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log N_{[~]}\left(\epsilon,~\mathcal{F}_{\Pi c^{\top}\psi}(B_{1:T-1},\Theta),~L_{2+\alpha}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\right)}d\epsilon<\infty
for any
\epsilon>0
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{\Pi c^{\top}\phi_{t}}(B_{1:t-1},K_{t})\triangleq\left\{\big[\prod_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\pi_{t^{\prime}}(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t^{\prime}-1})\big]c^{\top}\phi_{t}(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{t}\big)\textnormal{~s.t.~}\beta_{1:t-1}\in B_{1:t-1},\beta_{t}\in K_{t}\right\}
for any compact set
K_{t}\subseteq\real^{d_{t}}
and any
c\in\real^{d_{t}}
. By Lemma
A.5
, we have the following results:

(b1)

Under Condition
3
and assumption
(CP3)
,
\mathcal{F}_{\Pi c^{\top}\phi_{t}}\big(B_{1:t-1},K_{t})
has a finite bracketing number, i.e., that
N_{[~]}\left(\epsilon,~\mathcal{F}_{\Pi c^{\top}\phi_{t}}(B_{1:t-1},K_{t}),~L_{1+\alpha}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\right)<\infty
for any
\epsilon>0
.

(b2)

Under Condition
3
and assumption
(NP1)
,
\mathcal{F}_{\Pi c^{\top}\phi_{t}}\big(B_{1:t})
has a finite bracketing integral, i.e., that
\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log N_{[~]}\left(\epsilon,~\mathcal{F}_{\Pi c^{\top}\phi_{t}}(B_{1:t}),~L_{2+\alpha}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\right)}d\epsilon<\infty
for any
\epsilon>0
.

Proof of Lemma
A.5
.

For notational convenience, let

\pi_{2:t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{1:t-1})\triangleq\prod_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\pi_{t^{\prime}}\big(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)};\beta_{t^{\prime}-1}\big).

By Lemma
A.4
(Product of Lipschitz Policy Function are Lipschitz) for any
\beta_{1:t-1},\tilde{\beta}_{1:t-1}\in B_{1:t-1}
,

\big|\pi_{2:t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{1:t-1})-\pi_{2:t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{1:t-1})\big|\leq\dot{\pi}_{2:t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\big\|\beta_{1:t-1}-\tilde{\beta}_{1:t-1}\big\|_{2}

(A.14)

where
\dot{\pi}_{2:t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\triangleq\sum_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\dot{\pi}_{t^{\prime}}\big(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}\big)
. Note that by assumption
(i)
(that Condition
3
holds),
\|\dot{\pi}_{2:t}\|_{\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}},p}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\big[|\dot{\pi}_{2:t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})|^{p}\big]^{1/p}<\infty
.

Constructing bracketing functions that cover
\Pi_{2:t}
.

Let
\epsilon>0
. We now use the approach from Lemma 19.7 of
Van der Vaart 2000
. Since
B_{1:t-1}
is compact, the size of
B_{1:t-1}
in every fixed dimension is at most
\textnormal{diam}(B_{1:t-1})<\infty
. We can cover
B_{1:t-1}
with
\big[\textnormal{diam}(B_{1:t-1})/\epsilon\big]^{d_{1:t-1}}
or fewer cubes of with edges of size
\epsilon
; recall that
d_{1:t-1}\triangleq\sum_{t^{\prime}=1}^{t-1}d_{t^{\prime}}
. Let the projection of the centers of each of these finitely many cubes onto
B_{1:t-1}
be the points
\mathcal{B}_{1:t-1}\subset B_{1:t-1}
. Note that
|\mathcal{B}_{1:t-1}|=\big[\textnormal{diam}(B_{1:t-1})/\epsilon\big]^{d_{1:t-1}}
. For each of these cubes, consider the circumscribed ball that contains the cube; each of these balls has radius of
c\epsilon
for some constant
0<c<\infty
.

We now construct a collection of bracketing functions that cover
\Pi
. These bracketing functions are

\left\{\left[\pi_{2:t}\big(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{1:t-1}\big)-\epsilon\dot{\pi}_{2:t}(\,\cdot\,),~\pi_{2:t}\big(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{1:t-1}\big)+\epsilon\dot{\pi}_{2:t}(\,\cdot\,)\right]\right\}_{\beta_{1:t-1}\in\mathcal{B}_{1:t-1}}.

(A.15)

The above brackets are of size at most
2\epsilon\|\dot{\pi}_{2:t}\|_{\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}},p}
in
L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})
norm by assumption
(i)
(that Condition
3
holds).

We now discuss why the brackets from display (
A.15
) cover
\Pi_{2:t}
. Consider any
\beta_{1:t-1}\in B_{1:t-1}
. Since the grid of cubes that cover
B_{1:t-1}
whose projected centers form the collection of points
\mathcal{B}_{1:t-1}
have edges of length
\epsilon
, thus there must exists some
\beta_{1:t-1}^{(k)}\in\mathcal{B}_{1:t-1}
such that
\big\|\beta_{1:t-1}-\beta_{1:t-1}^{(k)}\big\|_{2}\leq\epsilon
. By display (
A.14
),

\pi_{2:t}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{1:t-1}\big)\in\big[\pi_{2:t}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{1:t-1}^{(k)}\big)-\epsilon\dot{\pi}_{2:t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)}),\pi_{2:t}\big(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{1:t-1}^{(k)}\big)+\epsilon\dot{\pi}_{2:t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\big]\textnormal{~~a.s.}

Thus, we have that

N_{[~]}\left(2\epsilon\|\dot{\pi}_{2:t}\|_{\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}},p},\Pi_{2:t}\cdot\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\right)\leq|\mathcal{B}_{1:t-1}|=\big[\textnormal{diam}(B_{1:t-1})/\epsilon\big]^{d_{1:t-1}}.

(A.16)

Additionally, note that
0\leq\pi_{2:t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{1:t-1})\leq 1
a.s. since this function is a product of probabilities. Thus the brackets from display (
A.15
) can be modified such that the bracketing functions are in
[0,1]
w.p.
1
while maintaining coverage of
\Pi_{2:t}
and not increasing the size of the brackets. Specifically, these brackets are:

\big\{\big(l_{\Pi,k},u_{\Pi,k}\big)\big\}_{k=1}^{|\mathcal{B}_{1:t-1}|}\triangleq\\
\bigg\{\bigg[\max\big\{0,\pi_{2:t}\big(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{1:t-1}\big)-\epsilon\pi_{2:t}(\,\cdot\,)\big\},\min\big\{1,\pi_{2:t}\big(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{1:t-1}\big)+\epsilon\pi_{2:t}(\,\cdot\,)\big\}\bigg]\bigg\}_{\beta_{1:t-1}\in\mathcal{B}_{1:t-1}}.

(A.17)

Constructing bracketing functions that cover
\Pi_{2:t}\cdot\mathcal{F}
.

By assumption
(ii)
, we can find
N_{\mathcal{F},\epsilon}\triangleq N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)<\infty
bracketing functions which cover
\mathcal{F}
. We will call these bracketing functions
\big\{\big[l_{\mathcal{F},k},u_{\mathcal{F},k}\big]\big\}_{k=1}^{N_{\mathcal{F},\epsilon}}
.
We now show that we can construct a finite collection of bracketing functions which cover
\Pi_{2:t}\cdot\mathcal{F}
using the bracketing functions for
\mathcal{F}
and
\Pi_{2:t}
.

Consider any function
\pi_{2:t}(\,\cdot\,;\beta_{1:t-1})f(\,\cdot\,)\in\Pi_{2:t}\cdot\mathcal{F}
.

•

From display (
A.17
), we can find some bracket
\big[l_{\Pi,k},u_{\Pi,k}\big]
for
k\in[1\colon|\mathcal{B}_{1:t-1}|]
such that
0\leq l_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\leq\pi_{2:t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{1:t-1})\leq u_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\leq 1
a.s.

•

Additionally, we can find some bracket
\big[l_{\mathcal{F},j},u_{\mathcal{F},j}\big]
for
k\in[1\colon N_{\mathcal{F},\epsilon}]
such that

l_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\leq f(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\leq u_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})
a.s.

Now note the following observations for any particular
\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)}
:

•

If for a particular
\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)}
,
f(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\geq 0
, then since
0\leq l_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\leq u_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\leq 1
, then

l_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\leq\pi_{2:t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{1:t-1})f(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\leq u_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)}).

•

If for a particular
\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)}
,
f(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})<0
, then since
0\leq l_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\leq u_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\leq 1
, then

u_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\leq\pi_{2:t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{1:t-1})f(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\leq l_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)}).

By the above two observations, we have that

\min\left\{l_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)}),u_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\right\}\leq\pi_{2:t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)};\beta_{1:t-1})f(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\\
\leq\max\left\{u_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)}),l_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\right\}\textnormal{~~a.s.}

Thus, the following bracketing functions cover
\Pi_{2:t}\cdot\mathcal{F}
:

\big\{\big(l_{\Pi_{2:t}\cdot\mathcal{F},k},u_{\Pi_{2:t}\cdot\mathcal{F},k}\big)\big\}_{k=1}^{|\mathcal{B}_{1:t-1}|\cdot N_{\mathcal{F},\epsilon}}\\
\triangleq\bigg\{\bigg[\min\big(l_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j},u_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}\big),\max\big(u_{\Pi,k}\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j},l_{\Pi,k}\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}\big)\bigg]\bigg\}_{k=1;j=1}^{k=|\mathcal{B}_{1:t-1}|;j=N_{\mathcal{F},\epsilon}}.

(A.18)

Note that there are
|\mathcal{B}_{1:t-1}|\cdot N_{\mathcal{F},\epsilon}
brackets above.

We now derive the size of the above brackets for
\Pi_{2:t}\cdot\mathcal{F}
.

\left|\max\big(u_{\Pi,k}\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j},l_{\Pi,k}\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}\big)-\min\big(l_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j},u_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}\big)\right|

Since
\big|\max(a,b)-c\big|\leq|a-c|+|b-c|
,

\leq\left|u_{\Pi,k}\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}-\min\big(l_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j},u_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}\big)\right|\\
+\left|l_{\Pi,k}\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}-\min\big(l_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j},u_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}\big)\right|\textnormal{~~a.s.}

\leq\left|u_{\Pi,k}\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}-l_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|+\left|u_{\Pi,k}\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}-u_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|\\
+\left|l_{\Pi,k}\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}-l_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|+\left|l_{\Pi,k}\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}-u_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|\textnormal{~~a.s.}

Since
0\leq u_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\leq 1
a.s. and
0\leq l_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\leq 1
a.s.,

=\left|u_{\Pi,k}\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}-l_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|+\left|l_{\Pi,k}\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}-u_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|\\
+u_{\Pi,k}\left|u_{\mathcal{F},j}-l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|+l_{\Pi,k}\left|u_{\mathcal{F},j}-l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|

Again since
0\leq u_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\leq 1
a.s. and
0\leq l_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\leq 1
,

\leq\left|u_{\Pi,k}\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}-l_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|+\left|l_{\Pi,k}\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}-u_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|+2\left|u_{\mathcal{F},j}-l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|\textnormal{~~a.s.}

By triangle inequality,

\leq\left|u_{\Pi,k}\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}-u_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|+\left|u_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}-l_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|\\
+\left|l_{\Pi,k}\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}-l_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|+\left|l_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}-u_{\Pi,k}\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|+2\left|u_{\mathcal{F},j}-l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|\textnormal{~~a.s.}

Using the same arguments as used above,

\leq\left|u_{\mathcal{F},j}-l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|+\left|u_{\Pi,k}-l_{\Pi,k}\right||l_{\mathcal{F},j}|+\left|u_{\mathcal{F},j}-l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|+\left|l_{\Pi,k}-u_{\Pi,k}\right||l_{\mathcal{F},j}|+2\left|u_{\mathcal{F},j}-l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|\textnormal{~a.s.}

=2\left|u_{\Pi,k}-l_{\Pi,k}\right||l_{\mathcal{F},j}|+4\left|u_{\mathcal{F},j}-l_{\mathcal{F},j}\right|

Thus,

\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\bigg[\bigg|\max\big\{u_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)}),l_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\cdot u_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\big\}\\
-\min\big\{l_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)}),u_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\cdot l_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\big\}\bigg|^{p}\bigg]

\leq\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\bigg[\bigg|2\big|u_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})-l_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\big||l_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})|+4\big|u_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})-l_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\big|\bigg|^{p}\bigg]

By Lemma
B.2
(Inequality using Binomial Theorem), for some constant
c_{p}<\infty
,

\leq 2^{p}c_{p}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\left[\left|u_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})-l_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\right|^{p}|l_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})|^{p}\right]\\
+4^{p}c_{p}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\left[\left|u_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})-l_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\right|^{p}\right]

Since brackets
[l_{\mathcal{F},j},u_{\mathcal{F},j}]
are of size
\epsilon
or less in
L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})
norm by construction,

\leq 2^{p}c_{p}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\left[\left|u_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})-l_{\Pi,k}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\right|^{p}\big|l_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\big|^{p}\right]+4^{p}c_{p}\epsilon^{p}

By the definition of brackets
[l_{\Pi,k},u_{\Pi,k}]
from display (
A.17
),

\leq 2^{p}c_{p}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\left[\left|2\epsilon\dot{\pi}_{2:t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\right|^{p}|l_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})|^{p}\right]+4^{p}c_{p}\epsilon^{p}

Recall that by assumption of the Lemma,
F
is a function such that
|f(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})|\leq F(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})
a.s. for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
. Thus, the brackets
\big[l_{\mathcal{F},k},u_{\mathcal{F},k}\big]
can always be chosen such that
|l_{\mathcal{F},j}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})|\leq F(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})
a.s. Thus,

\leq 2^{p}c_{p}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\left[\left|2\epsilon\dot{\pi}_{2:t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\right|^{p}|F(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})|^{p}\right]+4^{p}c_{p}\epsilon^{p}

Since
\dot{\pi}_{2:t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\geq 0
and
F(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\geq 0
by definition,

=2^{2p}c_{p}\epsilon^{p}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\left[\left|\dot{\pi}_{2:t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})F(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\right|^{p}\right]+4^{p}c_{p}\epsilon^{p}

Since
\dot{\pi}_{2:t}(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})=\sum_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\dot{\pi}_{t^{\prime}}\big(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}\big)
by definition,

=2^{2p}c_{p}\epsilon^{p}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\bigg[\bigg|\sum_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\dot{\pi}_{t^{\prime}}\big(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}\big)F(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\bigg|^{p}\bigg]+4^{p}c_{p}\epsilon^{p}

By repeatedly applying Lemma
B.2
(Inequality using Binomial Theorem), for some positive constant
k_{p}
,

=\epsilon^{p}\bigg\{2^{2p}c_{p}k_{p}^{t}\sum_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\left[\big|\dot{\pi}_{t^{\prime}}\big(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}\big)F(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\big|^{p}\right]+4^{p}c_{p}\bigg\}.

The term
2^{2p}c_{p}k_{p}^{t}\sum_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\left[\big|\dot{\pi}_{t^{\prime}}\big(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}\big)F(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\big|^{p}\right]+4^{p}c_{p}
above is bounded by assumption
(iii)
.

Let
c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}\triangleq\left\{2^{2p}c_{p}k_{p}^{t}\sum_{t^{\prime}=2}^{t}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{2:t}^{*}}\left[\left|\dot{\pi}_{t^{\prime}}\big(A_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)},S_{t^{\prime}}^{(i)}\big)F(\mathcal{H}_{t}^{(i)})\right|^{p}\right]+4^{p}c_{p}\right\}^{1/p}
.
By the above result, the brackets for
\Pi_{2:t}\cdot\mathcal{F}
from display (
A.18
) have size at most
\epsilon c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}
in
L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})
norm. Thus,

N_{[~]}\left(\epsilon c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}},\Pi_{2:t}\cdot\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\right)\leq\underbrace{|\mathcal{B}_{1:t-1}|}_{\textnormal{Upper~bounds~}N_{[~]}\left(\epsilon,\Pi_{2:t},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\right)}\cdot N_{\mathcal{F},\epsilon}\\
=\big[\textnormal{diam}(B_{1:t-1})/\epsilon\big]^{d_{1:t-1}}N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big).

The final equality above holds by display (
A.16
).

The above implies that

N_{[~]}\left(\epsilon,\Pi_{2:t}\cdot\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\right)\leq\big[\textnormal{diam}(B_{1:t-1})c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}/\epsilon\big]^{d_{1:t-1}}\cdot N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon/c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}},\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big).

(A.19)

Note that by assumption
(ii)
,
N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon/c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}},\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)<\infty
, so the above implies that
N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon,\Pi_{2:t}\cdot\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)<\infty
for all
\epsilon>0
.

Bracketing integral result.

We now work on showing the second part of the Lemma, i.e., that
\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon,\Pi_{2:t}\cdot\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)}d\epsilon<\infty
.

\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log N_{[~]}\left(\epsilon,\Pi_{2:t}\cdot\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\right)}d\epsilon

By display (
A.19
),

\leq\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log\left\{\big[\textnormal{diam}(B_{1:t-1})c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}/\epsilon\big]^{d_{1:t-1}}N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon/c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}},\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)\right\}}d\epsilon

Using properties of
\log
,

=\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{d_{1:t-1}\log\big[\textnormal{diam}(B_{1:t-1})c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}/\epsilon\big]+\log N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon/c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}},\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)}d\epsilon

Note that
\textnormal{diam}(B_{1:t-1})c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}/\epsilon\geq 1
since
\textnormal{diam}(B_{1:t-1})c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}/\epsilon
upper bounds the bracketing number
N_{[~]}\left(\epsilon,\Pi_{2:t},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\right)
(which must be at least
1
). Thus,
\log\big[\textnormal{diam}(B_{1:t-1})c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}/\epsilon\big]\geq 0
. Since
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
for any numbers
a,b>0
(to see this, square both sides of the inequality),

=\sqrt{d_{1:t-1}}\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log\big[\textnormal{diam}(B_{1:t-1})c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}/\epsilon\big]}d\epsilon+\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon/c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}},\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)}d\epsilon.

(A.20)

We now discuss why the quantity in the display above is finite:

•

Regarding the first term in display (
A.20
), note that since
\log\big[\textnormal{diam}(B_{1:t-1})c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}/\epsilon\big]\geq 0
, we have that
\log\big[\textnormal{diam}(B_{1:t-1})c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}/\epsilon\big]\leq\textnormal{diam}(B_{1:t-1})c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}/\epsilon
. Thus,

\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log\big[\textnormal{diam}(B_{1:t-1})c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}/\epsilon\big]}d\epsilon\leq\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\textnormal{diam}(B_{1:t-1})c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}/\epsilon}d\epsilon\\
=\sqrt{\textnormal{diam}(B_{1:t-1})c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}}\int_{0}^{1}\epsilon^{-2}d\epsilon<\infty.

•

For the second term in display (
A.20
) above, note that

\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon/c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}},\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)}d\epsilon=c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon/c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}},\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)}c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}^{-1}d\epsilon

By integration by substitution, for
u=\epsilon/c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}
,

=c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}\int_{0}^{c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}^{-1}}\sqrt{\log N_{[~]}\big(u,\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)}du<\infty.

(A.21)

The above term is finite since
\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log N_{[~]}\big(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)}d\epsilon<\infty
by assumption
(iv)
. If
c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\leq 1
display (
A.21
) holds straightforwardly. If
c_{\Pi\cdot\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\geq 1
, display (
A.21
) holds because
N_{[~]}\big(1,\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)\geq N_{[~]}\big(u,\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\big)
for all
u\geq 1
by the definition of bracketing numbers.

We have shown that
\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log N_{[~]}\left(\epsilon,\Pi_{2:t}\cdot\mathcal{F},L_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi^{*}})\right)}d\epsilon<\infty
, so we have now shown the second result of the Lemma.
~~\blacksquare

Appendix B
Policy Parameter Results

Overview of Supplement
B
Results.

B.1
:
Consistency of Policy Parameters (Sufficient Assumptions for Condition
1
; Theorem
B.1
)

B.2
:
Inequality Using Binomial Theorem (Helper Lemma
B.2
)

B.3
:
Stochastic Equicontinuity for Policy Parameters (Theorem
B.3
)
